## Page 1


A UNIVERSAL PROPERTY FOR GROUPOID C*-ALGEBRAS. II.
FELL BUNDLES
ALCIDES BUSS, ROHIT HOLKAR, AND RALF MEYER
Abstract. We define possibly unsaturated, upper semicontinuous Fell bundles
over Hausdorff, locally compact groupoids and establish a universal property
for representations of their full section C∗-algebras on Hilbert modules over
arbitrary C∗-algebras. Based on this, we prove that the full section C∗-algebra
is functorial and exact, and we define a quasi-orbit space and a quasi-orbit map.
We deduce and extend Renault’s Integration and Disintegration Theorems to
general Fell bundles using our universal property.

1. Introduction
Let G be a Hausdorff, locally compact groupoid with a Haar system α. The group-
oid C∗-algebra C∗(G, α) is characterised in [9] by a universal property. It describes
the representations of C∗(G, α) on Hilbert modules over arbitrary C∗-algebras and
thus determines C∗(G, α) uniquely. When combined with the representation theory
of commutative C∗-algebras, it implies the Integration and Disintegration Theorems
of Renault for representations of C∗(G, α) on a separable Hilbert space. Here we
extend this universal property to section C∗-algebras of Fell bundles over G. In
particular, our universal property covers crossed products for actions and partial
actions of G. While the basic ideas are the same as in [9], the Fell bundles cause
some new difficulties.
Fell bundles over G are defined in Section 3. Our technical assumptions are as
mild as conceivable at this time: we allow upper semicontinuous fields of C∗-algebras
and unsaturated Fell bundles. Thus our Fell bundles are more general than in
previous works. Renault [51] only considers continuous fields of C∗-algebras as
opposed to upper semicontinuous fields, and he considers only twisted actions,
not Fell bundles; in particular, the Fell bundles associated to twisted actions are
always saturated. Yamagami [55] does allow unsaturated Fell bundles, but does not
give details on the basic theory of Fell bundles and their C∗-algebras. Muhly and
Williams [45] allow upper semicontinuous fields throughout, but require Fell bundles
to be separable and saturated. Since basic results of the theory are established in
different sources under slightly different assumptions, we develop the theory of Fell
bundles over groupoids from scratch here.
Our theory is not yet as general as possible because we only consider Hausdorff
groupoids. The non-Hausdorff case adds further complications. These are of a
different nature and will be treated in a separate article.
A Fell bundle has Banach spaces (Ag)g∈G as fibres.
These form an upper
semicontinuous field of Banach spaces and carry continuous families of involutions
Ag →Ag−1 and multiplication maps
Ag × Ah →Agh,
(a, b) 7→a · b,
for (g, h) ∈G2 := G ×s,G0,r G; here, G0 denotes the space of objects of G. The
multiplication maps satisfy the standard algebraic properties of a Fell bundle and
a positivity condition for a∗· a for all a ∈Ag and g ∈G (see Definition 3.4). The
restriction of A to G0 is an upper semicontinuous field of C∗-algebras on G0, which
we denote by A. Let A := C0(G0, A) be the C0(G0)-C∗-algebra of sections of A.
Since a Hilbert bimodule is a partial Morita–Rieffel equivalence, we interpret the
Fell bundle A as a generalised partial action of G on A.
A partial action of G on A consists of isomorphisms θg : Dg−1
∼
−→Dg for certain
ideals Dg ⊆Ar(g).
These ideals must depend continuously on G in the sense
that there is an ideal D ◁r∗(A) with fibres Dg ◁Ar(g); here r∗(A) denotes the
pullback of A to a C0(G)-C∗-algebra along r: G →G0. In addition, we require
that Du(x) = Ax for all x ∈G0 and that θg ◦θh ⊆θgh as partial maps. This is the
groupoid generalisation of a partial group action as defined in [25]. An action of G


## Page 3


A UNIVERSAL PROPERTY FOR GROUPOID C*-ALGEBRAS. II. FELL BUNDLES
3
is a partial action where Dg = Ar(g) for all g ∈G. Any partial action of G defines a
Fell bundle, which is saturated if and only if the partial action is an action.
Section 4 defines representations of a Fell bundle on a Hilbert module F over an
arbitrary C∗-algebra D. As in [9], this is based on C∗-correspondences associated
to open continuous surjections r, s: G ↠G0 and d0, d1, d2 : G2 ↠G and families of
measures along their fibres induced by the Haar system on G. In the case of Fell
bundles, these C∗-correspondences acquire “coefficients” related to the Fell bundle.
That is, instead of scalar-valued L2-functions we consider L2-sections of various
bundles. A representation on a Hilbert module F has two pieces. One piece is a
representation of the C∗-algebra A associated to the Fell bundle. The other piece is
a unitary operator between certain Hilbert D-modules that are built by tensoring F
over A with certain Hilbert A-modules. This is subject to a condition that is
formulated using the space G2 of composable pairs. There are two new technical
issues compared to [9]. Namely, adding coefficients to the C∗-correspondences and
restricting to appropriate Hilbert submodules to get a unitary operator even if the
action of G on A is only partial.
The continuous, compactly supported sections of a Fell bundle form a ∗-algebra
Cc(G, A). Section 5 integrates a representation of A on a Hilbert module F to a
representation of Cc(G, A) on the same Hilbert module F. By construction, this
integrated representation is bounded by a variant of the I-norm.
Section 6 disintegrates a representation of Cc(G, A) on F to a representation
of A on F.
As in [9], this also works for representations by densely defined
unbounded operators, assuming only some weak continuity in the inductive limit
topology on Cc(G, A). Thus any densely defined representation of Cc(G, A) that is
continuous in the inductive limit topology extends to a representation by adjointable
operators that is bounded for the I-norm on Cc(G, A).
The integration and disintegration processes are inverse to each other. Thus there
is a maximal C∗-norm on Cc(G, A) that is continuous in the inductive limit topology;
and this C∗-norm is already bounded by the I-norm. The full section C∗-algebra
C∗(G, A) of the Fell bundle is the completion of Cc(G, A) in this C∗-norm. Our
analysis shows that representations of C∗(G, A) on a Hilbert module F are equivalent
to Fell-bundle representations of A on F. This is our universal property for C∗(G, A).
Since it covers representations on arbitrary Hilbert modules, it determines C∗(G, A)
uniquely up to isomorphism.
We also define the reduced section C∗-algebra C∗
r(G, A) of a Fell bundle A
over G, which is based on a “regular representation” of the Fell bundle A. We
show that the regular representation generates a norm on Cc(G, A) – not just a
seminorm. Thus the canonical maps from Cc(G, A) to C∗
r(G, A) and C∗(G, A) are
injective. This implies that the canonical ∗-homomorphisms A →M(C∗(G, A)) and
A →M(C∗
r(G, A)) are injective.
In Section 7, we prove several expected results about section C∗-algebras of Fell
bundles. We describe the universal representation of the Fell bundle in C∗(G, A).
We prove that both full and reduced crossed products are functorial for ∗-homo-
morphisms of Fell bundles – defined suitably. And we show that these functors
preserve surjections and that the reduced crossed product preserves injections. We
also prove that the full crossed product functor is exact. For this, we study ideals
in Fell bundles and relate them to invariant ideals in A. We use split extensions to
define homomorphisms from a Fell bundle to the “multipliers” of another Fell bun-
dle. We show that these induce ∗-homomorphisms C∗(G, A) →M(C∗(G, B)) and
C∗
r(G, A) →M(C∗
r(G, B)). In the end of Section 7, we discuss approximate units
for the section C∗-algebras of general Fell bundles. Although our main theorems
are proven without relying on approximate units, we include this construction for


## Page 4


4
ALCIDES BUSS, ROHIT HOLKAR, AND RALF MEYER
future convenience. To the best of our knowledge, the existence of such approximate
units is currently only documented in the literature for saturated and separable Fell
bundles (see [45, Proposition 5.1]).
In Section 8, we build a dual action on the space bA of equivalence classes of
irreducible representations of A. We show that the open subsets in bA invariant
under the dual action correspond precisely to the Fell ideals in A. We prove that
the “restriction” of an ideal in C∗(G, A) to A is always invariant in this sense. Using
this result and the machinery developed in [39], we define a quasi-orbit space bA/∼
and a quasi-orbit map
\
C∗(G, A) →bA/∼.
Quasi-orbit spaces are a key ingredient in the Effros–Hahn Conjecture (see [22,31]).
They are somewhat implicit in the groupoid version of this conjecture in [52], and
interesting objects in their own right (compare [30]).
In Section 9, we study Fell-bundle representations on a separable Hilbert space.
The C0(G0)-C∗-algebra structure on A allows us to analyse a representation of A
through a measurable field of representations of the field A. Based on this, we
deduce a version of Renault’s Integration–Disintegration Theorem from our universal
property. We also bring in results of Ramsay in order to improve statements that
initially hold only almost everywhere so that they hold everywhere.
The last, rather short sections establish results that are specific to certain classes
of Fell bundles. In Section 10, we consider Fell bundles coming from twisted partial
actions. We show that measurable Fell-bundle representations on a Hilbert space
are equivalent to measurable covariant representations, and we also decompose
representations on Hilbert modules as “covariant representations” provided the
action is global and the twist is scalar-valued.
In Section 11, we consider Fell line bundles. These are often described through
circle bundles. We show that representations of a Fell line bundle are equivalent
to representations of the corresponding circle bundle that are homogeneous in a
suitable sense. We prove this both for the measurable representations on a separable
Hilbert space and for Fell-bundle representations on Hilbert modules.
In Section 12, we specialise to Fell bundles over groups. In this case, there is
a rather obvious concept of a continuous Fell-bundle representation on a Hilbert
module. We prove that these continuous Fell-bundle representations are equiva-
lent to our Fell-bundle representations. For representations on Hilbert space and
under separability assumptions, our results imply that a measurable Fell-bundle
representation is almost everywhere equal to a continuous Fell-bundle representation.
Section 13 considers Fell bundles over transformation groupoids X ⋊G. Here a
Fell bundle B over X ⋊G induces a Fell bundle A over G by forgetting the fibrewise
decomposition over X. We show that the Fell bundles B over X ⋊G and A over G
have the same Fell-bundle representations and thus have canonically isomorphic
section C∗-algebras.
2. Upper semicontinuous fields of Banach spaces
The main new issue in this article compared to [9] is that we must add fields
of C∗-algebras or C∗-correspondences as coefficients to various constructions in [9].
In this section, we recall some definitions and results needed for this. We recall
upper semicontinuous fields of Banach spaces, C∗-algebras, C∗-correspondences,
and Hilbert bimodules. We build a C∗-correspondence between the C∗-algebras of
C0-sections of two upper semicontinuous fields of C∗-algebras from a topological
correspondence decorated by an upper semicontinuous field of C∗-correspondences,
and we prove some lemmas about these constructions.

A UNIVERSAL PROPERTY FOR GROUPOID C*-ALGEBRAS. II. FELL BUNDLES

Let X be a locally compact space. An upper semicontinuous field of Banach
spaces over X is a family of Banach spaces Bx for x ∈X with a topology on the total
space B = F
x∈X Bx satisfying, among others, the following properties (see [20] or
[54, Appendix C] for the full definition): the addition and scalar multiplication are
continuous maps B ×X B →B and C × B →B, respectively, and the norm function
B →[0, ∞) is upper semicontinuous. Here B ×X B denotes the fibre product over X,
consisting of all pairs (a, b) ∈B × B with p(a) = p(b), where p: B →X denotes
the bundle projection. We often describe the topology on B by giving the space
C0(X, B) of continuous sections X →B vanishing at infinity. There is a unique
topology on B with the specified continuous sections by [8, Proposition 2.4].
An upper semicontinuous field of C∗-algebras over X is a family of C∗-algebras Bx
for x ∈X with a topology on B := F Bx making it an upper semicontinuous
field of Banach spaces and making the fibrewise multiplication and involution
continuous maps B ×X B →B and B →B, respectively. There is a bijection between
isomorphism classes of upper semicontinuous fields of C∗-algebras over X and of
C0(X)-C∗-algebras (see [46]), mapping a field B to its C∗-algebra of C0-sections
C0(X, B).
Let A and B be C∗-algebras. An A-B-correspondence is a Hilbert B-module E
with a nondegenerate representation of A by adjointable operators, A →B(E). A
Hilbert A-B-bimodule is a vector space E that is both a right Hilbert B-module and
a left Hilbert A-module, such that the structures are compatible in the sense that
the actions of A and B commute and ξ · ⟨η | ζ⟩= ⟨⟨ξ | η⟩⟩· ζ for all ξ, η, ζ ∈E. Here
⟨· | ·⟩and ⟨⟨· | ·⟩⟩denote the right B- and left A-valued inner products, respectively.
Such a Hilbert A-B-bimodule is, in particular, an A-B-correspondence; conversely,
an A-B-correspondence comes from a Hilbert A-B-bimodule if and only if the
left action φ: A →B(E) restricts to an isomorphism φ|I : I
∼
−→K(E) between
some (twosided) ideal I ◁A and the ideal K(E) of compact operators in B(E) (see
[10, Lemma 4.2]). Then the left inner product on E is the composite of the standard
K(E)-valued inner product and φ|−1
I . This ideal I and the left inner product on E
are unique if they exist. Namely, I must be Katsura’s ideal φ−1(K(E)) ∩ker φ,

A correspondence isometry between two A-B-correspondences E and F is an
A-B-bimodule homomorphism f : E →F that preserves the right inner product,
that is, ⟨f(ξ) | f(η)⟩= ⟨ξ | η⟩for all ξ, η ∈E. Then f is an isometry, but need not be
adjointable. A Hilbert bimodule map between two Hilbert A-B-bimodules E and F
is an A-B-bimodule homomorphism f : E →F that preserves both inner products,
that is, ⟨f(ξ) | f(η)⟩= ⟨ξ | η⟩and ⟨⟨f(ξ) | f(η)⟩⟩= ⟨⟨ξ | η⟩⟩for all ξ, η ∈E. Thus it is
a correspondence isometry. All correspondence isometries in our universal property
are invertible because we will restrict the codomains suitably.
Let A = (Ax)x∈X and B = (Bx)x∈X be two upper semicontinuous fields of
C∗-algebras over X. An upper semicontinuous field of C∗-correspondences between
them is a family of Ax-Bx-correspondences Ex with a topology on E = (Ex)x∈X
that makes it an upper semicontinuous field of Banach spaces such that the left
and right multiplications and the inner product are continuous maps A ×X E →E,
E ×X B →E and E ×X E →B, respectively. We also ask the norms on the fibres Ex
that make E an upper semicontinuous field to induced by the inner products, that
is, ∥ξ∥Ex = ∥⟨ξ | ξ⟩∥1/2
Bx for all x ∈X, ξ ∈Ex.
An upper semicontinuous field of Hilbert bimodules between A and B is a family
of Ax-Bx-Hilbert bimodules with a topology that makes it an upper semicontinuous
field of C∗-correspondences and also makes the left inner product continuous. The

An important way to build new upper semicontinuous fields of Banach spaces is
the pull-back construction. Let X and Y be locally compact spaces, f : X →Y a
continuous map, and B = (By)y∈Y an upper semicontinuous field of Banach spaces
over Y . Then there is an upper semicontinuous field of Banach spaces f ∗(B) over X
with fibre Bf(x) at x ∈X and with the fibre product topology on the total space
F
x∈X Bf(x) = X ×Y
F
y∈Y By. The space of C0-sections of f ∗(B) is the closed linear
span of sections of the form x 7→h(x)b(f(x)) for h ∈C0(X) and b ∈C0(Y, B).
The pull-back of an upper semicontinuous field of C∗-algebras is again an upper
semicontinuous field of C∗-algebras. The pull-back of an upper semicontinuous field
of C∗-correspondences or Hilbert bimodules between two upper semicontinuous fields
of C∗-algebras A and B is an upper semicontinuous field of C∗-correspondences or
Hilbert bimodules between f∗(A) and f ∗(B), respectively.
In the following, we usually abbreviate “upper semicontinuous field” to “field”,
that is, all fields of Banach spaces, C∗-algebras or C∗-correspondences over locally
compact spaces are tacitly assumed to be upper semicontinuous.
We sometimes have to prove that two fields of Banach spaces are isomorphic.
When we already have an isometric map between them, then this is a fibrewise issue:
Lemma 2.1. Let A and B be fields of Banach spaces over X and let ψ: A →B be
a map between them that is fibrewise isometric. In other words, let A be a subfield
in the field of Banach spaces B. The map ψ is an isomorphism of fields of Banach
spaces if and only if ψx has dense range for all x ∈X.
Since ψ is isometric, ψx is surjective when its range is dense. So the density
condition in the lemma is weaker than surjectivity; we use this weaker form in later
applications.
Proof. It is clear that ψx has dense range if ψ is an isomorphism. Conversely,
assume this. Since the topology of a field of Banach spaces is determined by the
continuous sections, it is enough to prove that any C0-section h of B is the ψ-image
of a C0-section of A. We are going to find C0-sections fε of A for ε > 0 such that
∥ψ(fε) −h∥∞< ε. Then the sections fε form a Cauchy net converging towards a
ψ-preimage of h because
∥fε1 −fε2∥= ∥ψ(fε1) −h −(ψ(fε2) −h)∥< 2 max{ε1, ε2}.
Fix ε > 0. For each x ∈X, there is a continuous section fx of A with ∥ψ(fx(x))−
h(x)∥< ε. Since the norm function on B is upper semicontinuous and ψ(fx) −h is a
continuous section, there is a neighbourhood Ux of x such that ∥ψ(fx(y))−h(y)∥< ε
for all y ∈Ux. There is also a compact subset K ⊆X such that ∥h(y) −0∥< ε
for y /∈K. Cover K by finitely many open subsets of the form Ux. Adding the
complement of K gives a finite cover of X. Using a partition of unity subordinate
to this finite cover, we build a C0-section fε of A with ∥ψ(fε(y)) −h(y)∥< ε for all
y ∈X.
□
Definition 2.2. Let X be a locally compact space and let B = (Bx)x∈X be a field
of Banach spaces over X. A family of closed subspaces Ax ⊆Bx is continuous if
for each x ∈X, a ∈Ax, there is a continuous section f of B with f(x) = a and
f(y) ∈Ay for all y ∈X.
Lemma 2.3. Let Ax ⊆Bx for x ∈X be a continuous family of subspaces. Then
there is a unique field A of Banach spaces with fibres Ax such that a continuous
section of A is the same as a continuous section of B with values in A. The implied
topology on A is the subspace topology from B.

Lemma 2.4. Let X be a locally compact space, let B = (Bx)x∈X be a field of Banach
spaces over X, and let Ax ⊆Bx be closed subspaces such that for each x ∈X, the
set of f(x) ∈Ax for continuous sections f of B with f(y) ∈Ay for all y ∈X is
dense in Ax. Then the field of subspaces (Ax)x∈X is continuous.
Proof. Let a ∈Ax. We must build a continuous section f as in Definition 2.2.
By assumption, there are continuous sections fn of B with fn(y) ∈Ay for all
y ∈X and with ∥fn(x) −a∥< 2−n for all n ∈N. Then the set of y ∈X with
∥fn(y) −fn+1(y)∥< 2−n−1 is an open neighbourhood of x. There are hn ∈C0(X)
that are supported in this open neighbourhood with ∥hn∥∞= 1 and hn(x) = 1 for
all n ∈N. Then the series f1(y)+P∞
n=1 hn(y)(fn+1(y)−fn(y)) converges uniformly
towards a continuous section f of B with the required properties, that is, f(y) ∈Ay
for all y ∈X and f(x) = f1(x) + (a −f1(x)) = a.
□
Remark 2.5. Let X be a locally compact space and let B = (Bx)x∈X be a field of
C∗-algebras over X. A continuous family of ideals in B is equivalent to an ideal
in C0(X, B). In one direction, an ideal J ◁C0(X, B) gives a continuous family
of ideals by Ix := evx(J) for all x ∈X. Here evx : C0(X, B) →Bx denotes the
evaluation map. The ideals Ix determine J uniquely. In the other direction, the
space of C0-sections of a continuous family of ideals is an ideal in C0(X, B). These
two constructions are inverse to each other. (See also Corollary 7.11 for a more
general statement involving hereditary Fell subbundles.)
Let E : A →B and F : B →D be C∗-correspondences. Their composite is the
(balanced) tensor product E ⊗B F (see [41, Chapter 4]). This is the completion of
the algebraic (balanced) tensor product E ⊙B F with respect to the D-valued inner
product
⟨ξ1 ⊗η1 | ξ2 ⊗η2⟩:= ⟨η1 | φ(⟨ξ1 | ξ2⟩)η2⟩,
where the homomorphism φ: B →B(F) gives the left B-module structure of F.
There is a similar operation for fields of C∗-correspondences. Let A, B and D be
fields of C∗-algebras over X and let E and F be fields of C∗-correspondences between
them. Then there is a unique field of C∗-correspondences over X between A and D
whose space of C0-sections is the closed linear span of sections of the form ξ ⊗η for
ξ ∈C0(X, E), η ∈C0(X, F). We denote this field of C∗-correspondences by E ⊗B F.

Lemma 2.8. With the notation above, the map
γ : Cc(X, E)⊙Cc(Y, F) →Cc(X, E ⊗f ∗(B) f ∗(F)),
γ(φ⊗ψ)(x) := φ(x)⊗ψ(f(x)),
extends uniquely to an isomorphism of C0(X, A)-C0(Z, D)-correspondences

Proof. The proof is exactly the same as for [9, Lemma 2.3]. Easy computations show
that the map γ is a C0(X, A)-C0(Z, D)-bimodule homomorphism that preserves
the C0(Z, D)-valued inner products. The topology on f ∗(F) is defined so that
sections of the form ψ(g(x))h(x) with g ∈Cc(Y, F) and h ∈Cc(X) span a dense
subspace of Cc(X, f ∗(F)) with respect to the inductive limit topology.
Since
γ(h · φ ⊗ψ)(x) = h(x)φ(x) ⊗ψ(g(x)) = φ(x) ⊗h(x)ψ(g(x)) and sections of the
form h(x)ψ(g(x)) span a dense subspace of Cc(X, f ∗F), it follows that γ has dense
image. Thus the isometry γ is unitary.
□

We shall later need that certain Hilbert modules built from L2-sections are full.
This will follow from the following lemma:
Lemma 2.11. Let X and Y be locally compact Hausdorff spaces, f : X →Y a
continuous map, B = (By)y∈Y a field of C∗-algebras over Y , E = (Ex)x∈X a field
of Hilbert modules over f ∗B, and λ = (λy)y∈Y a continuous family of measures
along f with full support. The Hilbert C0(Y, B)-module L2(X, E, f, λ) is full if, for
every y ∈Y , the linear span of {⟨Ex | Ex⟩: x ∈f −1(y)} is dense in By.
Proof. Recall that L2(X, E, f, λ) is the completion of Cc(X, E) in the norm associated
to the C0(Y, B)-valued inner product

The Hilbert C0(Y, B)-module L2(X, E, f, λ) is isomorphic to the space of C0-sections
of the field of Hilbert By-modules L2(f −1(y), E, λy).
Lemma 2.1 implies that
L2(X, E, f, λ) is full if and only if L2(f −1(y), E, λy) is full as a Hilbert By-module
for every y ∈Y . So we only need to prove the lemma in the case where Y is a point.
In other words, we must check that L2(X, E, λ) is full as a Hilbert B-module if B

3. Fell bundles over groupoids
Fell bundles over groups have been introduced in [20,28] and were generalised
to groupoids in [37,55]. They are treated in depth in [45]. Unlike the Fell bundles
in [45], ours need not be saturated, their fibres need not be separable, and G need
not be second countable. Let G be a locally compact, Hausdorff groupoid. As usual,
we also write G for its arrow space. Let G0 be its object space, s, r: G ⇒G0 the
source and range maps, and u: G0 →G the unit map. Throughout the paper, we
identify units x ∈G0 with their identity arrows u(x) ∈G, and we often write x

(3.2)

Definition 3.4. A Fell bundle over G is an upper semicontinuous field of Banach
spaces A = (Ag)g∈G with bilinear maps Ag × Ah →Ag·h, (a, b) 7→a · b, for
(g, h) ∈G2 (multiplication) and conjugate-linear maps Ag →Ag−1, a 7→a∗, for
g ∈G (involution) with the following properties:
(1) (a · b) · c = a · (b · c) for all (g, h, k) ∈G3, a ∈Ag, b ∈Ah, c ∈Ak;
(2) (a · b)∗= b∗· a∗for all (g, h) ∈G2, a ∈Ag, b ∈Ah;
(3) (a∗)∗= a for all g ∈G, a ∈Ag;
(4) ∥a · b∥≤∥a∥· ∥b∥for all (g, h) ∈G2, a ∈Ag, b ∈Ah;
(5) ∥a∗a∥= ∥a∥2 for all g ∈G, a ∈Ag;
(6) for each g ∈G, a ∈Ag, there is b ∈Au(s(g)) with b∗b = a∗a;
(7) the multiplication and involution are continuous as maps A ×G0 A →A
and A →A, respectively, where
A ×G0 A := {(a, b) ∈Ag × Ah : g, h ∈G, s(g) = r(h)} ⊆A × A.
Let AgAh ⊆Agh denote the closed linear span of products a · b for a ∈Ag, b ∈Ah.
The Fell bundle is called saturated if AgAh = Agh for all (g, h) ∈G2.
Conditions (1)–(5) for g = h = k = u(x) with x ∈G0 say that Au(x) with its
multiplication, involution and norm is a C∗-algebra. Thus the pullback u∗A of A for
the inclusion map u: G0 ,→G, that is, the restriction of A to G0, becomes an upper
semicontinuous field of C∗-algebras over G0. Throughout the article, we denote this
field by A := u∗A, its fibres by Ax := Au(x) for x ∈G0, and we let A := C0(G0, A).
If g ∈G, then we equip Ag with the products Ar(g) ×Ag →Ag and Ag ×As(g) →
Ag and the left and right inner products ⟨⟨a | b⟩⟩:= a · b∗and ⟨a | b⟩:= a∗· b. This

and the left one because ⟨⟨a | b⟩⟩= ⟨a∗| b∗⟩. Condition (3) implies that the involution

Remark 3.6. Let n ∈N and let (g1, . . . , gn) be a chain of composable arrows. Then
we often tacitly identify the Hilbert bimodule tensor product
Ag1Ag2 · · · Agn := Ag1 ⊗As(g1) Ag2 ⊗As(g2) · · · ⊗As(gn−1) Agn
with its image under the iterated multiplication map to Ag1·g2···gn. The iterated
multiplication map is an isometry of Hilbert bimodules. It does not depend on how
we put parentheses, that is, it is associative. Let Gn be the space of composable
n-chains in G. The Hilbert bimodules Ag1Ag2 · · · Agn and Ag1·g2···gn are the fibres of
continuous fields of Hilbert bimodules over Gn, and the iterated multiplication map
identifies the former with a subfield of the latter. We may also write A∗

(see [50, Proposition 1.4]). Therefore, the completion of Cc(G, A) with respect to
∥·∥I is a Banach ∗-algebra, denoted L1
I(A) or L1
I(G, A).
Definition 3.15. The full section C∗-algebra C∗(G, A) = C∗(G, A, α) of A is the
enveloping C∗-algebra of L1
I(A).
Equivalently, C∗(A) is the (Hausdorff) completion of Cc(G, A) in the maximal
C∗-seminorm on Cc(G, A) with ∥ξ∥≤∥ξ∥I for all ξ ∈Cc(G, A). This seminorm is,
in fact, a norm (see Proposition 5.21). Our main results imply that this norm is
also the maximal C∗-seminorm that is continuous in the inductive limit topology.
3.2. Twisted partial actions and Fell bundles. Fell bundles over groupoids
contain other kinds of generalised actions of groupoids as special cases. The most
basic notion is that of ordinary (global) actions by automorphisms. These have
been introduced by Le Gall [29]. He defines a continuous action of G as a C0(G0)-
C∗-algebra A with an isomorphism of C0(G)-C∗-algebras a: s∗A
∼
−→r∗A such that
agh = agah for all (g, h) ∈G2; we use unusual symbols for actions because α and β
usually denote Haar systems in this article. We turn the C0(G0)-C∗-algebra A
into an upper semicontinuous field of C∗-algebras A = (Ax)x∈G0 as usual (see [46]).
Namely, Ax := A/(C0(X \ {x}) · A) and A = C0(G0, A). Let A := r∗A be the
pullback along the range map. This is a field of C∗-algebras over G with fibres Ar(g).
To distinguish elements of Ag and Ar(g), we write aδg ∈Ag for a ∈Ar(g). The
product and involution on A are defined by
(aδg) · (bδh) := a · ag(b) δgh,
(aδg)∗:= ag−1(a∗) δg−1

Remark 3.21. Let G be a locally compact group. Then it is common to study
measurable twisted actions (see, for instance, [6]). Exel and Laca show in [26] that
measurable twisted actions of G also give continuous Fell bundles over G. We are
not aware of a generalisation of this to groupoids. It is clear that mere measurability
is not enough because we need continuity of the bundle on G0.
Definition 3.22. A twisted partial action of a locally compact groupoid G has the
following data:
(1) an (upper semicontinuous) field of C∗-algebras A = (Ax)x∈G0 over G0;
(2) ideals Dg ◁Ar(g) for all g ∈G;
(3) isomorphisms ag : Dg−1
∼
−→Dg for all g ∈G;
(4) unitary multipliers w(g, h) of Dg ∩Dgh for all (g, h) ∈G2.
We require the following conditions:
(5) Du(x) = Ax and au(x) = idAx for all x ∈G0, and w(g, u(s(g))) = 1 and
w(u(r(g)), g) = 1 for all g ∈G;
(6) ag(Dg−1 ∩Dh) ⊆Dgh for all (g, h) ∈G2;
(7) ag(ah(a)) = w(g, h)agh(a)w(g, h)∗for (g, h) ∈G2 and a ∈ah−1(Dg−1 ∩Dh);
(8) ag(a · w(h, k)) · w(g, hk) = ag(a) · w(g, h) · w(gh, k) for all (g, h, k) ∈G3 and
a ∈Dg−1 ∩Dh∩Dhk; for this formula to make sense, we restrict multipliers of
ideals to smaller ideals, so as to turn w(h, k) ∈U(Dh ∩Dhk) into a multiplier

Dg ∩Dgh in r∗(A).
A partial action is defined as a twisted partial action where all unitaries w(g, h) are
the unit multiplier of the appropriate ideal. A twisted action is a twisted partial
action with Dg = Ar(g) for all g ∈G. Twisted partial actions with w(g, h) = 1 for
all (g, h) ∈G2 and Dg = Ar(g) for all g ∈G are ordinary actions of G.
Partial actions of discrete groups were introduced by McClanahan in [42]. Exel
extended this definition to locally compact groups and added twists in [23]. Some
algebraists have considered partial actions of discrete groupoids on rings (see [3–5]).
Anantharaman-Delaroche [2] defined partial actions of topological groupoids and
assigned Fell bundles to them. The definitions in [2,23] are special cases of ours.

Example 3.32 (Restriction of actions). Let B = (Bx)x∈G0 be an upper semicontinuous
field of C∗-algebras over G0 and let b: s∗(B)
∼
−→r∗(B) be an action of G on B. Let
A = (Ax)x∈G0 be a continuous family of ideals in B. For g ∈G, let
Dg := {b ∈Ar(g) : b−1
g (b) ∈As(g)}.
This is a continuous family of ideals in r∗(B). The automorphism bg maps Dg−1
isomorphically onto Dg. Let ag be the restriction of bg to Dg−1. These domains and
automorphisms define a partial action of G on A called the restriction of b to A.
We may restrict a twisted or a twisted partial action in the same way to a twisted
partial action.
Example 3.33 (Green–Renault twists). Green’s twisted actions for groups (see [32])
have been generalised to groupoids by Kumjian [36] and Renault [51]. These differ
from the twisted actions above, which generalise Busby–Smith twisted actions
(see [6]). Renault uses an extension of locally compact groupoids S ↣Σ ↠G,


## Page 20


20
ALCIDES BUSS, ROHIT HOLKAR, AND RALF MEYER
where S is a bundle of Abelian groups acted upon by G. A twisted action of G is
an action of Σ that is implemented by specified unitaries on S, which satisfy the
conditions for a crossed module representation as in [11,13,14]. A Green–Renault
twisted action as above gives rise to a Fell bundle over G in a canonical way. This
is explained in detail in [45, Example 2.5 and discussion thereafter], and it is also
related to the construction of Fell bundles in [11,12,14].
In summary, Fell bundles encompass twisted partial actions, twisted actions, and
ordinary actions as special cases. Saturated Fell bundles may be interpreted as
actions by Morita equivalences. For groups, this is explained in [14]; see also [10] for
the context of inverse semigroup actions. So we interpret unsaturated Fell bundles
as partial actions by partial Morita equivalences.
The following theorem characterises which Fell bundles come from twisted partial
actions and twisted actions. It generalises analogous criteria for groups in [23] and
for inverse semigroups in [7].
Theorem 3.34. Let G be a Hausdorff, locally compact groupoid. A Fell bundle A
over G is isomorphic to the Fell bundle defined by a twisted partial action if and only
if A is isomorphic to a continuous field of ideals in r∗A as a left Hilbert r∗A-module;
equivalently, A is isomorphic to a continuous field of ideals in s∗A as a right Hilbert
s∗A-module. A Fell bundle is isomorphic to the Fell bundle defined by a twisted
action if and only if A is isomorphic to r∗A as a field of left Hilbert r∗A-modules, if
and only if A is isomorphic to s∗A as a field of right Hilbert s∗A-modules.
Proof. We may use the inversion in G to switch between the left and right Hilbert
module structures. Therefore, it suffices to prove the left-handed versions of the
two claims. By construction, the Fell bundle associated to a twisted partial action
is isomorphic to a continuous field of ideals in r∗A as a left Hilbert r∗A-module.
And the Fell bundle associated to a twisted action is isomorphic to r∗A as a left
Hilbert r∗A-module. Conversely, let A be a Fell bundle that is isomorphic to a
continuous field of ideals (Dg)g∈G in r∗A as a field of left Hilbert r∗A-modules.
Without loss of generality, we assume Ag = Dg. Then Dg = AgA∗
g = AgAg−1 as
ideals in Ag. Since Ag is a Hilbert bimodule, the right multiplication action of As(g)
on Ag restricts to an isomorphism from the ideal ⟨Ag | Ag⟩= Ag−1Ag = Dg−1 onto
the C∗-algebra of compact operators on the left Hilbert Ar(g)-module Dg. The latter
is canonically isomorphic to Dg. This gives an isomorphism ag : Dg−1
∼
−→Dg. These
isomorphisms depend continuously on g. By Lemma 2.1, they form an isomorphism
of continuous families of ideals a: inv∗(D)
∼
−→D.
Let (g, h) ∈G2. There are canonical isomorphisms
Ag ⊗As(g) Ah = AgA∗
gAg ⊗As(g) Ah
∼= Ag ⊗As(g) A∗
gAgAh ∼= Dg · ag(Dg−1 · Dh) = ag(Dg−1 ∩Dh).
They map a ⊗b with a ∈Dg, b ∈Dg−1 · Dh to a · ag(b). The product mg,h in the
Fell bundle gives another Hilbert bimodule map from Ag ⊗As(g) Ah to Agh = Dgh.
Two ideals that are isomorphic as left Hilbert modules are equal because the ideal is
also the range ideal of the left scalar product. So the two maps above have the same
image. Hence ag(Dg−1 ∩Dh) ⊆Dgh. As in Lemma 3.23, it follows that the common
image is Dg ∩Dgh. Since the left Hilbert module structure is the obvious one, there
must be a unitary multiplier w(g, h) of Dg ∩Dgh with mg,h(a⊗b) = a·ag(b)·w(g, h)
for all a ∈Dg, b ∈Dg−1 ∩Dh. This gives the data of a twisted partial action. The
proof above shows that the ideals Dg form a continuous family of ideals, that (ag) is
an isomorphism inv∗D
∼
−→D, and that right multiplication by w(g, h) for (g, h) ∈G2
preserves continuity of sections.

21
We now verify the algebraic conditions for a twisted partial action in Defini-
tion 3.22. They follow from the associativity of the multiplication in the Fell bundle.
The computations are similar to those in [7,23] for Fell bundles over groups and
inverse semigroups.
For x ∈G0, Au(x) = Ax implies Du(x) = Au(x)A∗
u(x) = Ax. Since Au(x) is the
trivial Hilbert bimodule Ax, its right multiplication coincides with the usual one,
giving au(x) = idAx. Condition (6) says that ag(Dg−1 ∩Dh) ⊆Dgh, which was
already shown above.
We denote the Fell-bundle multiplication as ◦and express it in terms of a and w.
The definition of w(g, h) through the isomorphism mg,h implies
(3.35)
x ◦y = x · ag(y) · w(g, h)
for all x ∈Ag and y ∈Dg−1 ∩Dh ⊆Ah; here the product on the right is in
Agh ∼= Dgh. Similarly, the identification of the right action of As(g) on Ag with the
action by compact operators gives x ◦a = x · ag(a) for x ∈Ag and a ∈Dg−1. This
gives the normalisation condition in (5) in Definition 3.22 and finishes the proof
of (5).
To check condition (7), let (g, h) ∈G2. Let x ∈Ag = Dg, y ∈Dg−1 ∩Dh ⊆Ah,
and let a ∈Dh−1 ∩D(gh)−1 = ah−1(Dg−1 ∩Dh), viewed as an element of As(h). Since
the Fell bundle is associative, we may expand (x ◦y) ◦a = x ◦(y ◦a) in two ways.
First, since a ∈Dh−1 ∩D(gh)−1 and y ∈Dh ∩Dg−1, (3.35) implies
(x ◦y) ◦a = (x ◦y) · agh(a) = x · ag(y) · w(g, h) · agh(a).
Similarly, we compute
x ◦(y ◦a) = x ◦(y · ah(a)) = x · ag(y · ah(a)) · w(g, h) = x · ag(y) · ag(ah(a)) · w(g, h).
Equating both sides gives
x · ag(y) · w(g, h) · agh(a) = x · ag(y) · ag(ah(a)) · w(g, h).
As x and y vary, the elements x · ag(y) span the ideal ag(Dg−1 ∩Dh) = Dg ∩Dgh.
Thus, w(g, h) · agh(a) = ag(ah(a)) · w(g, h) as multipliers of Dg ∩Dgh. Multiplying
by w(g, h)∗on the right yields condition (7) in Definition 3.22.
To prove condition (8), let (g, h, k) ∈G3. Let x ∈Ag, y ∈Dg−1 ∩Dh ⊆Ah, and
take z ∈Dh−1 ∩D(gh)−1 ∩Dk ⊆Ak. By Lemma 3.23, ah restricts to a bijection
from Dh−1 ∩D(gh)−1 ∩Dk to Dg−1 ∩Dh ∩Dhk.
Thus, as y and z vary, the elements a := y · ah(z) span the ideal Dg−1 ∩Dh ∩Dhk.
We compute (x ◦y) ◦z and x ◦(y ◦z). Our assumptions on x, y, z imply that all
relevant products are computed through (3.35). On the one hand,
(x◦y)◦z = x·ag(y)·w(g, h)·agh(z)·w(gh, k) = x·ag(y)·ag(ah(z))·w(g, h)·w(gh, k),
where the second equation uses Condition (7) for z ∈Dh−1 ∩D(gh)−1. On the other
hand,
x◦(y◦z) = x·ag(y·ah(z)·w(h, k))·w(g, hk) = x·ag(y)·ag(ah(z))·ag(w(h, k))·w(g, hk).
We may cancel x because these elements span Dg, and find
ag(a) · w(g, h) · w(gh, k) = ag(a) · ag(w(h, k)) · w(g, hk) = ag(a · w(h, k)) · w(g, hk).
Since a spans Dg−1 ∩Dh ∩Dhk, this proves condition (8).
□

4. Representations of Fell bundles on Hilbert modules
Let G be a locally compact groupoid with Haar system α, and let A be a Fell
bundle over G. Let D be a C∗-algebra and let F be a Hilbert D-module. We are
going to define representations of A on F, generalising the definition in [9] without
Fell bundles. Adding the coefficient bundles requires some careful choices in various
definitions, especially when the Fell bundle fails to be saturated. It also requires
extra steps in most proofs, which check new aspects related to the bundles that
have been added.
A representation of the groupoid G on F is defined in [9] as a pair (φ, U), where φ
is a nondegenerate ∗-homomorphism C0(G0) →B(F) and U is a unitary operator
L2(G, s, ˜α) ⊗φ F →L2(G, r, α) ⊗φ F that intertwines the left actions of C0(G); in
other words, φ makes F into a C0(G)-D-correspondence and U is an isomorphism
of C0(G)-D-correspondences. It is required, in addition, that two isomorphisms
of C0(G2)-D-correspondences L2(G2, v2, µ0) ⊗φ F ⇒L2(G2, v0, µ0) ⊗φ F that are
built from U are equal. We now add the Fell bundle A to this definition.
The natural replacement for C0(G0) is C0(G0, A), the C∗-algebra of C0-sections
of A. So now φ is a nondegenerate ∗-homomorphism
φ: C0(G0, A) →B(F)
that makes F a C0(G0, A)-D-correspondence.
We replace L2(G, s, ˜α) and L2(G, r, α) by L2(G, A, s, ˜α) and L2(G, AA∗, r, α),
respectively. Here A is viewed as a field of AA∗-s∗A-correspondences and AA∗as
a field of AA∗-r∗A-correspondences; in particular, each fibre Ag of A is an AgA∗
g-
As(g)-correspondence and each fibre AgA∗
g of AA∗is an AgA∗
g-Ar(g)-correspondence.
The correspondences L2(G, A, s, ˜α) and L2(G, AA∗, r, α) are defined in Section 2.
Recall that L2(G, A, s, ˜α) is the completion of Cc(G, A) in the norm associated to
the C0(G0, A)-valued inner product

The Hilbert modules L2(G, A, s, ˜α) and L2(G, AA∗, r, α) also carry natural left
multiplication actions of the larger C∗-algebra C0(G, r∗A). Asking U to intertwine
the left actions of C0(G, r∗A) or C0(G, AA∗) makes no difference:
Lemma 4.2. Both L2(G, A, s, ˜α) and L2(G, AA∗, r, α) are nondegenerate as left
modules over C0(G, AA∗). A unitary operator U as above that intertwines the left
actions of C0(G, AA∗) also intertwines the left actions of C0(G, r∗A).
Proof. The multiplication map C0(G, AA∗) ⊗C0(G,r∗A) C0(G, A) →C0(G, A) is a
fibrewise isomorphism by Lemma 3.7, and then it is an isomorphism of fields of
Banach spaces by Lemma 2.1. So the multiplication map C0(G, AA∗) ⊗C0(G,r∗A)
L2(G, A, s, ˜α) →L2(G, A, s, ˜α) has dense range. It is also isometric, hence unitary.

Thus L2(G, A, s, ˜α) is a nondegenerate left module over C0(G, AA∗). The same
argument works for L2(G, AA∗, r, α). A nondegenerate representation ϱ of an ideal I
in a C∗-algebra B extends uniquely to a representation ϱ′ of B. If a unitary U is
an intertwiner for ϱ, then ϱ′ and AdU ◦ϱ′ are two extensions of ϱ to B. Hence they
are equal. This means that U is also an intertwiner for ϱ′. This implies the second
claim.
□

The point of this definition is the following universal property, which is analogous
to [9, Theorem 3.23]:
Theorem 4.6. Let G be a locally compact Hausdorff groupoid with Haar system α,
and let A be a Fell bundle over G. Let D be a C∗-algebra and F a Hilbert D-module.
There is a bijection between nondegenerate representations of the C∗-algebra C∗(G, A)
on F and representations of (G, A, α) on F. This bijection is natural in the following
two ways:
(1) Let F1 and F2 be two Hilbert D-modules and let V : F1 ,→F2 be an isometry.
Then it intertwines two representations of C∗(G, A) on F1 and F2 if and
only if it intertwines the corresponding representations of (G, A, α).
(2) Let E be a C∗-correspondence from D to a C∗-algebra D′. A representation
of C∗(G, A) or of (G, A, α) on F induces a representation of the same type
on F ⊗D E. The bijections between the two types of representations on F
and F ⊗D E are compatible with these induction processes.
This characterises C∗(G, A) uniquely up to canonical isomorphism.
We will prove this in Sections 5 and 6.
5. Integration of representations

Lemma 5.12 and (5.11) imply (5.9). And this finishes the proof of Proposition 5.8.
As a result, any representation of a Fell bundle A integrates to an I-norm bounded,
nondegenerate representation of the ∗-algebra Cc(G, A). This extends uniquely to a
nondegenerate representation of C∗(G, A) by the universal property.
5.1. The regular representation. We are going to define a “regular” repre-
sentation of the Fell bundle A and show that it integrates to the usual regular
representation, namely, the action of Cc(G, A) on L2(G, A, s, ˜α) by convolution. As
a consequence, the latter representation of Cc(G, A) is bounded in the I-norm. The
constructions below are the same as in [9]. We merely add appropriate continuous
fields of C∗-algebras and C∗-correspondences everywhere. We construct the regular
representation on dense subspaces of continuous sections of compact support. In
this form, the construction will reappear in the proof of the disintegration theorem
in Section 6.
The regular representation (φ, U) of A is defined on the Hilbert C0(G0, A)-module
F := L2(G, A, s, ˜α). Defining φ on F amounts to making it a C0(G0, A)-C0(G0, A)-
correspondence. We simply take the C∗-correspondence r∗L2(G, A, s, ˜α) that is
defined by the decorated topological correspondence

The regular representation defines the reduced C∗-algebra of A:
Definition 5.22. The reduced section C∗-algebra C∗
r(G, A) of A is the image
of C∗(A) in the regular representation. Equivalently, it is the completion of Cc(G, A)

If ϱ is faithful, then Ind ϱ is faithful because the homomorphism
B(L2(G, A, s, ˜α)) →B(L2(G, A, s, ˜α) ⊗C0(G0,A) E),
T 7→T ⊗idE,

More generally, let I be a set whose elements are representations
ϱi : C0(G0, A) →B(Ei) on certain Hilbert modules Ei. Assume that this set of
representations is faithful in the sense that T
i∈I ker ϱi = {0}. Then the family
of induced representations Ind ϱi is faithful in the same sense. This is because
{ϱi : i ∈I} is faithful if and only if L
i∈I ϱi is faithful. In particular, the family of
restriction maps C0(G, A) →Ax for x ∈G0 is faithful. It follows that the reduced
norm defining C∗
r(G, A) is equal to a supremum over G0, where the norm at x
is the norm in the regular representation of C∗(G, A) in the Hilbert Ax-module
L2(Gx, A|Gx, ˜αx); this is the fibre of L2(G, A, s, ˜α) at x. We could also use the
family of all irreducible representations of A or, equivalently, of the fibres Ax for
x ∈X. If the field of C∗-algebras A is continuous and X0 ⊆X is a dense subset,
then the family of restriction maps for x ∈X0 is faithful as well. Thus the reduced
norm is also equal to a supremum over X0 ⊆X. These and similar alternative
descriptions of the reduced C∗-norm are used in the literature for saturated Fell
bundles (see [24,43,53]).
6. Disintegration of representations
We are going to “disintegrate” a representation L: Cc(G, A) →B(F) to a rep-
resentation (φ, U) of (G, A, α). This disintegration still works for some densely
defined “unbounded representations”, showing that all such representations are in
fact bounded. This works exactly as in [9], and the required notion of “unbounded
representation” is a literal translation of [9, Definition 5.1]. Recall the subspaces
C0(K \ ∂K, A) of Cc(G, A) defined in Definition 5.3.
Definition 6.1. Let D be a C∗-algebra and let F be a Hilbert D-module. A pre-
representation of Cc(G, A) on F consists of a vector space F0, a linear map ι: F0 →F
with dense image, and a bilinear map L: Cc(G, A) × F0 →F, (f, ξ) 7→L(f)ξ, such
that
(1) if ξ, η ∈F0 and K ⊆G is compact, then the map C0(K \ ∂K, A) →D,
f 7→⟨ι(ξ) | L(f)η⟩D, is bounded in norm; roughly speaking, L is continuous
for the inductive limit topology on Cc(G, A) and a suitable weak topology
on the space of linear maps F0 →F;
(2) ⟨L(f1)ξ | L(f2)η⟩D = ⟨ι(ξ) | L(f ∗
1 ∗f2)η⟩D for ξ, η ∈F0, f1, f2 ∈Cc(G, A);

Our first goal is to define a nondegenerate representation φ: Cc(G0, A) →B(F)
from a pre-representation of Cc(G, A). Let B be the C∗-algebra of all bounded
sections of the field of multiplier algebras (M(Ax))x∈G0 with the property that
pointwise multiplication with them maps C0(G0, A) again to C0(G0, A). In fact, B
is naturally isomorphic to the multiplier algebra of C0(G0, A); but we shall not use
this fact. The C∗-algebra B is unital and contains C0(G0, A). It acts on Cc(G, A)
by multipliers through
(b † f)(g) := b(r(g)) · f(g),
(f ‡ b)(g) := f(g) · b(s(g))
for all b ∈B, f ∈Cc(G, A), g ∈G. Easy computations show that these multiplica-
tion maps are bilinear and satisfy (b † f)∗= f ∗‡ b∗and
(f1 ‡ b) ∗f2 = f1 ∗(b † f2),
(b1 · b2) † f = b1 † (b2 † f).
for all f, f1, f2 ∈Cc(G, A), b1, b2, b ∈B. The properties b † (f1 ∗f2) = (b † f1) ∗f2,
f ‡ (b1 · b2) = (f ‡ b1) ‡ b2 and (f1 ∗f2) † b = f1 ∗(f2 ‡ b) follow from this, and they
are also easy to check directly.

Lemma 6.13. Let (φ, U) be a representation of (G, A, α) on a Hilbert module F.
Its integral L: Cc(G, A) →B(E) uniquely determines (φ, U).

determines φ.

Now the proof of Theorem 4.6 is finished exactly as in [9]. We have integrated a
representation of (G, A, α) to a representation of Cc(G, A) that is bounded in the
I-norm; hence it extends uniquely to a representation of the C∗-algebra C∗(G, A).
Conversely, we have disintegrated a representation of C∗(G, A) to a representation of
(G, A, α). Proposition 6.11 shows that integration after disintegration is the identity
map on representations of C∗(G, A). And Lemma 6.13 shows that integration is
injective. So disintegration and integration are inverse to each other. The naturality
properties in Theorem 4.6 are rather evident; it is left to the reader to transfer this
part of the proof of Theorem 3.23 in [9].
We are going to derive several consequences of our results in the following sections.
Here is an immediate one:
Corollary 6.14. Let A be a Fell bundle over a locally compact groupoid with Haar
system (G, α) and let D be a C∗-algebra. A ∗-homomorphism φ: Cc(G, A) →D is
continuous in the inductive limit topology if and only if it is bounded in the I-norm.
In that case it extends uniquely to a ∗-homomorphism C∗(G, A) →D.
Proof. By definition, the homomorphism φ extends to C∗(G, A) if and only if it is
bounded in the I-norm; such an extension, if it exists, is necessarily unique. If φ is
bounded in the I-norm, then it is continuous in the inductive limit topology.
Conversely, suppose that φ is continuous in the inductive limit topology. Then
the formula ∥f∥:= ∥φ(f)∥D defines a C∗-seminorm on Cc(G, A). Let D′ denote the
Hausdorff completion of Cc(G, A) with respect to this seminorm. By construction,
φ descends to an isometric ∗-homomorphism D′ →D, identifying D′ with the
C∗-subalgebra φ(Cc(G, A)) ⊆D generated by the image of φ.
The canonical map Cc(G, A) →D′ — namely, the co-restriction of φ — is
nondegenerate (this is precisely why we replaced D with D′), and hence defines
a pre-representation of Cc(G, A) on D′. As such, it is bounded in the I-norm by
Corollary 6.12, and this bound is inherited by φ.
□
7. Functoriality of full and reduced section C∗-algebras
In this section, we prove some rather basic results about section C∗-algebras of
Fell bundles over groupoids. Throughout this section, let (G, α) be a locally compact
groupoid with Haar system and let A be a Fell bundle over G. Let A := A|G0 be the
restriction of A to units. This is a field of C∗-algebras over G0. Let A := C0(G0, A)
be the corresponding C0(G0)-C∗-algebra.


## Page 40


40
ALCIDES BUSS, ROHIT HOLKAR, AND RALF MEYER
We are going to describe a universal Fell-bundle representation on C∗(G, A). It
contains a canonical ∗-homomorphism A →M(C∗(G, A)). We show that the latter
is injective. Then we study homomorphisms of Fell bundles. These induce homo-
morphisms of full and reduced section C∗-algebras. It is easy to see that a surjection
of Fell bundles induces a surjection on full and reduced section C∗-algebras and
that an injection of Fell bundles induces an injection of reduced section C∗-algebras.
An injection of Fell bundles need not induce an injective ∗-homomorphism on the
full section C∗-algebras: this already fails for crossed products by group actions.
We define hereditary and ideal Fell subbundles and show that C∗(G, I) is naturally
isomorphic to a C∗-ideal in C∗(G, A) if I is an ideal Fell subbundle in A and the
quotient corresponds to the quotient Fell bundle A/I. In other words, the canonical
maps
C∗(G, I) →C∗(G, A) →C∗(G, A/I)
form an extension of C∗-algebras.
7.1. The universal representation. The second part of the universal property,
Theorem 4.6.(2), implies that the bijection between representations of the Fell bundle
and its section C∗-algebra is induced by a universal representation, which we are now
going to describe. Briefly, it is the Fell-bundle representation on B := C∗(G, A, α)
that corresponds to the identity representation of B on itself under the bijection
in Theorem 4.6. In the following, we usually omit α from our notation and write
B = C∗(G, A). The identity map of B is a nondegenerate representation of B on
itself, where we view B as a Hilbert module over itself. We call the disintegrated form
of the identity representation the universal representation of the Fell bundle (A, G).
It is a pair
ι: A →M(B),
Uuniv : L2(G, A, s, ˜α) ⊗ι B
∼
−→L2(G, AA∗, r, α) ⊗ι B,
where ι is a nondegenerate ∗-homomorphism and Uuniv is a unitary operator. The
action of the C∗-algebra B on the Hilbert B-module B in the identity representation
B →B is the left multiplication in the C∗-algebra B. Therefore, in the disintegration
of the identity representation of B, Lemma 6.2 implies
(7.1)
ι(f)(g)(x) = f(r(x))g(x)
for f ∈C0(G0, B) and g ∈Cc(G, A).
The proof of disintegration shows that the unitary Uuniv is the unique extension
of the map U0 in (5.19).
By the naturality of the universal property, the universal representation (ι, Uuniv)
helps in computing the disintegrations of representations of B. Suppose that D is a

7.2. Functoriality. We now fix a locally compact Hausdorff groupoid G with a
Haar system and study morphisms between Fell bundles over G. Such morphisms
are given by fibrewise linear maps that are compatible with the multiplication,
involution, and the underlying groupoid structure.
Throughout this section, we fix a Fell bundle A over G. To discuss functoriality,
we consider a second Fell bundle B over G. We write B := B|G0 for the restriction
of B to the unit space and B := C0(G0, B).
Definition 7.3. A homomorphism of Fell bundles A →B is a continuous family of
linear maps τg : Ag →Bg for all g ∈G such that
τg−1(a∗) = τg(a)∗
and
τg(a) · τh(b) = τgh(a · b)
for all (g, h) ∈G2, a ∈Ag, b ∈Ah. Here “continuity” means that g 7→τg(ξ(g)) is a
continuous section of B whenever ξ is a continuous section of A. The homomorphism
is called injective or surjective if all maps τg are injective or surjective, respectively.
Remark 7.4. Let τ = (τg)g∈G be a homomorphism of Fell bundles. If x ∈G0,
then τu(x) : Ax →Bx is a ∗-homomorphism, hence norm-contractive. Then τg is
norm-contractive for all g ∈G because ∥a∥= ∥a∗a∥1/2 for a ∈Ag. The same idea
shows that (τg)g∈G is injective if and only if the ∗-homomorphisms τu(x) are injective
for all x ∈G0. In this case, each τg is isometric.

7.3. Hereditary Fell subbundles and exactness for Fell ideals.
Definition 7.7. Let B be a Fell bundle over G. A Fell subbundle is a continuous
family of Banach subspaces Ag ⊆Bg as in Definition 2.2 that satisfies A∗
g ⊆Ag−1
and Ag · Ah ⊆Agh for all (g, h) ∈G2. A Fell subbundle A ⊆B is hereditary if
AgBhAk ⊆Aghk for all (g, h, k) ∈G3; here G3 is defined in (3.2). It is a Fell ideal
if AgBh ⊆Agh and BgAh ⊆Agh for all g, h ∈G.
The two conditions AgBh ⊆Agh and BgAh ⊆Agh for all g, h ∈G are equivalent
for a Fell subbundle because A∗
g = Ag−1 for all g ∈G.
Remark 7.8. An injective Fell-bundle homomorphism i: A →B is the same as
an isomorphism of Fell bundles onto a Fell subbundle i(A) ⊆B. Fell ideals are
hereditary Fell subbundles.
By Lemma 2.3, a Fell subbundle is a Fell bundle in its own right, such that
the continuous sections of A are exactly those continuous sections f : G →B with
f(g) ∈Ag for all g ∈G. It is well known that any closed two-sided ideal in a
C∗-algebra is a ∗-ideal. This is proven using approximate units. The same argument
shows that the condition A∗
g = Ag−1 is redundant for Fell ideals:

Ig · Ah ⊆Ig·h,

for all (g, h) ∈G2. Then I∗

module. This makes it nondegenerate as a right Ius(g)-module. That is, lim x·ei = x

Recall that B := B|G0 and B := C0(G0, B).
Proposition 7.10. Let B be a Fell bundle over G. Let Ax ⊆Bx for x ∈G0 be a
continuous family of hereditary subalgebras. Let Ag ⊆Bg be Ar(g)BgAs(g), the closed
linear span of products a1b2a3 with a1 ∈Ar(g), b2 ∈Bg, a3 ∈As(g). Then (Ag)g∈G
is a hereditary Fell subbundle of B. Conversely, any hereditary Fell subbundle is
of this form for a unique continuous family of hereditary subalgebras Ax ⊆Bx for
x ∈G0. So there is a natural bijection between hereditary Fell subbundles in B and
continuous families of hereditary subalgebras in B.
Proof. It is easy to see that the family of Banach subspaces (Ar(g)BgAs(g))g∈G in B
is continuous. We check
(Ar(g)BgAs(g))∗= Ar(g−1)Bg−1As(g−1),
(Ar(g)BgAs(g)) · (Ar(h)BhAs(h)) ⊆Ar(g)BghAs(h),
(Ar(g)BgAs(g)) · Bh · (Ar(k)BkAs(k)) ⊆Ar(g)BghkAs(k)
for all (g, h, k) ∈G3. This says that (Ar(g)BgAs(g))g∈G is a hereditary Fell subbundle
of B. Since each Ax is hereditary in Bx, it follows that AxBxAx is equal to Ax. Thus
(Ar(g)BgAs(g))g∈G determines (Ax)x∈G0.
Now let (Ag)g∈G be any hereditary Fell subbundle of B.
If x ∈G0, then
Ax := Au(x) is a hereditary subalgebra in Bx. Since (Ag)g∈G is a Fell bundle in
its own right, Ag is nondegenerate as a bimodule over Ar(g) and As(g). That is,
Ag = Ar(g)AgAs(g) ⊆Ar(g)BgAs(g). The converse inclusion Ar(g)BgAs(g) ⊆Ag
follows from the definition of a hereditary Fell subbundle. Thus Ag = Ar(g)BgAs(g)
for all g ∈G.
□
Corollary 7.11. Hereditary Fell subbundles of B are in bijection with hereditary
C∗-subalgebras of B.
Proof. Proposition 7.10 describes a bijection between hereditary Fell subbundles
of B and continuous families of hereditary C∗-subalgebras Ax ⊆Bx for x ∈G0. Then
the subalgebra of A consisting of those sections with values in (Ax) is a hereditary
subalgebra of B.
Conversely, let D ⊆B be hereditary.
Then C0(G0) · D =
C0(G0) · D · D = D · C0(G0) · D ⊆D because D is hereditary and C0(G0) is central.
Thus D is also a C0(G0)-C∗-algebra. It follows that there is a continuous family of
C∗-subalgebras Ax ⊆Bx for x ∈G0 such that D = C0(G0, (Ax)x∈G0). Since D is
hereditary, it follows easily that each fibre Ax of D is hereditary in Bx.
□

hereditary C∗-subalgebra in ˜B := C0(G, BB∗).

This implies that (Ar(g)BgAs(g))g∈G is both a left and a right ideal in B, and this
makes it a Fell ideal. Conversely, if the subspaces Ag := Ar(g)BgAs(g) form a Fell

Ar(g)Bg = Au(r(g))Bg ⊆Ag = Ar(g)BgAs(g) ⊆Ar(g)BgBs(g) = Ar(g)Bg.

Definition 7.14. Let B be a Fell bundle over G and let A = (Ax)x∈G0 be a
continuous family of ideals in B = B|G0. We call A B-invariant if Ar(g)Bg = BgAs(g)
for all g ∈G.
Lemma 7.15. A continuous family of ideals A = (Ax)x∈G0 is B-invariant if and
only if BgAs(g)Bg−1 ⊆Ar(g) for all g ∈G.
Proof. If Ar(g)Bg = BgAs(g), then BgAs(g)Bg−1 = Ar(g)BgBg−1 ⊆Ar(g). Conversely,
assume BgAs(g)Bg−1 ⊆Ar(g). Then BgAs(g) ⊆BgAs(g)Bg−1Bg ⊆Ar(g)Bg. Taking
the adjoint of this relation for g−1 gives the reverse inclusion as well.
□
Corollary 7.16. A continuous family of ideals A is B-invariant if and only if the
hereditary subbundle in B that corresponds to A is a Fell ideal in B. As a consequence,
Fell ideals in B are in bijection with B-invariant ideals in B = C0(G0, B), which are
in turn in bijection with continuous families of B-invariant ideals of B.
Example 7.17. Assume B is the Fell bundle associated to an ordinary action of G
by a continuous family of isomorphisms βg : Bs(g)
∼

continuous family over the units A ⊆B is B-invariant if and only if it is invariant
in the usual sense of actions, that is, βg(As(g)) ⊆Ar(g) for all g ∈G; this follows
quickly from the definition of the product in B. As a consequence, Fell ideals of B
correspond to invariant ideals in the usual sense. If A ⊆B is any continuous family
of ideals, then the associated hereditary Fell subbundle is the one associated to the
partial action, given by restricting the action β to A.

Remark 7.20. We strongly believe that Proposition 7.19 remains true for arbitrary
hereditary Fell subbundles. In the special case of Fell bundles over discrete groups,
an analogous result is already known; see [25, Theorem 21.13]. However, the proof
technique used above for ideal Fell subbundles does not extend easily to arbitrary
hereditary Fell subbundles over groupoids. Although related methods can be applied
to certain ‘invariant’ hereditary subbundles, developing the necessary technical
framework would substantially complicate the exposition.
For this reason, we
restrict ourselves to ideal Fell subbundles and leave the general hereditary case for
future work.

Definition and Lemma 7.21. Let I ⊆A be a Fell ideal. There is a unique
Fell-bundle structure on the quotients Bg := Ag/Ig such that the quotient maps
Ag →Bg form a surjective Fell-bundle homomorphism. We call this the quotient
Fell bundle of A by I.
Proof. By assumption, I = (Ig)g∈G is a continuous family of closed subspaces of
the Banach spaces Ag. For each g ∈G, form the quotient Bg := Ag/Ig. Since
the quotient maps Ag →Bg and elements of Bg lift to elements with almost the
same norm, the family (Bg)g∈G becomes an upper semicontinuous Banach bundle
over G as well, such that the quotient maps assemble to a continuous open surjection
A →B.
The Fell-bundle structure on B is defined fibrewise by
[ag] · [bh] := [agbh],
[ag]∗:= [a∗
g],
for ag ∈Ag, bh ∈Ah.
This is well defined because I is a Fell ideal.
Thie
multiplication and involution on B are continuous because they are so on A and the
map A →B is a quotient map. The Fell-bundle axioms for B are inherited directly
from those of A.
□
This construction is standard in the literature. For saturated and separable Fell
bundles, a closely related quotient construction is carried out in [33,35]. We do not
need to assume the bundles to be saturated or separable.
Proposition 7.22. Let A be a Fell bundle over G, let I ◁A be a Fell ideal, and

C∗(G, A). Its image is an ideal because Cc(G, I) is an ideal in Cc(G, A). Proposi-
tion 7.5 shows that the quotient map A ↠B induces a surjective ∗-homomorphism
π: C∗(G, A) ↠C∗(G, B). The kernel of π contains ι(C∗(G, I)) because Cc(G, I)
is dense in C∗(G, I) and the composite map Cc(G, I) →Cc(G, A) →Cc(G, B)
vanishes. It remains to prove, conversely, that ker π is contained in ι(C∗(G, I)). For
this, we are going to build a Fell-bundle representation of B on
D := C∗(G, A)/ι(C∗(G, I)).
Let Q: C∗(G, A) ↠D be the quotient homomorphism. It corresponds to a Fell-
bundle representation (ψ, V ) of A on D by our universal property. Here ψ is a
(nondegenerate) ∗-homomorphism ψ: A →M(D) and V is a unitary operator
V : L2(G, A, s, ˜α) ⊗ψ D
∼
−→L2(G, AA∗, r, α) ⊗ψ D,
where D is viewed as a Hilbert D-module.
By naturality, ψ is the composite
of the canonical ∗-homomorphism A →M(C∗(G, A)) in Proposition 7.2 with Q.
This clearly vanishes on the ideal I ◁A. So it factors through a nondegenerate

˜ψ: A/I ∼= B →M(D).
In general, let A and D be C∗-algebras, I ◁A, let E be a Hilbert A-module,

The above extension generalises the classical case for saturated and separable Fell

Remark 7.25. We briefly compare our definition of a multiplier of a Fell bundle
with another candidate, which only works in the saturated case. Let (mh)h∈Gs(g)
be a multiplier of the Fell bundle A of degree g ∈G. Lemma 7.24 implies that
mus(g) : As(g) →Ag is an adjointable operator of right Hilbert As(g)-modules. Con-
versely, let T : As(g) →Ag be such an adjointable operator. If h ∈Gs(g), then T
induces an adjointable operator T ⊗As(g) idAh : Ah ∼= As(g) ⊗As(g) Ah →Ag ⊗As(g) Ah.
The multiplication in A gives an isometry Ag ⊗As(g) Ah ,→Agh. If A is saturated,
then this is unitary, hence adjointable. So T is part of a family of adjointable
operators Ah →Agh for all h ∈Gs(g). This is a multiplier of the Fell bundle. So
we may identify multipliers of degree g with B(As(g), Ag) if A is saturated.
If, however, the Fell bundle is not saturated, then the multiplication maps may
fail to be adjointable. Then we do not know whether the linear maps Ah →Agh
induced by T are adjointable. If, however, g = u(x) for x ∈G0 is a unit, then the
multiplication map is unitary even if the Fell bundle is not saturated. So in this
case, multipliers are just elements of B(Ax, Ax) ∼= M(Ax). That is, a multiplier of
degree u(x) is equivalent to a multiplier of the C∗-algebra Ax.

In Section 7.5 we shall give an alternative existence proof using split extensions,
which applies to all multiplier morphisms without appealing to the disintegration
theory and arrives at the same integrated map C∗(G, τ).
7.5. Multiplier homomorphisms as split extensions. We are going to rein-
terpret multiplier homomorphisms as split extensions of Fell bundles. This will
allow us to generalise some results from ordinary Fell-bundle homomorphisms to
multiplier homomorphisms. We first recall the analogous results for homomorphisms
A →M(B) between two C∗-algebras.
Definition 7.36. Let A and B be C∗-algebras. A split extension of A by B is an
extension B ↣E
p↠A of C∗-algebras with a ∗-homomorphism σ: A →E such
that p ◦σ = idA. A split extension of Fell bundles is defined similarly, replacing
C∗-algebras and ∗-homomorphisms by Fell bundles and Fell-bundle homomorphisms.
The following result is well known (see [47]), but we add the proof here for
convenience of the reader and also because we are going to use a similar idea for
Fell bundles.
Lemma 7.37. Let B and A be C∗-algebras. There is a natural bijection between
equivalence classes of split extensions of A by B and ∗-homomorphisms A →M(B).
Proof. Let a split extension as above be given with splitting section σ: A →E. Then
τ : A →M(B) defined by τ := R ◦σ is a ∗-homomorphism, where R: E →M(B),
R(x)b := x · b, is the canonical (restriction) homomorphism obtained from B viewed
as an ideal of E. Conversely, let τ : A →M(B) be a ∗-homomorphism. Let
E := {(a, m) ∈A ⊕M(B) : τ(a) −m ∈B}.
This is a C∗-subalgebra of A ⊕M(B). The map B →E, b 7→(0, b), identifies B
with an ideal in E, and the map E →A, (a, b) 7→a, identifies the quotient of E by
that ideal with A. The quotient map splits by the map A →E, a 7→(a, τ(a)). Thus
we get a split extension. The homomorphism A →M(B) induced by it is the given
homomorphism τ. Conversely, let us start with an extension B ↣E ↠A with a
section σ: A →E. Define τ as above, and then turn τ back into a split extension
B ↣Eτ ↠A. Then Eτ →E, (a, b) 7→σ(a) + b, is an isomorphism Eτ ∼= E that


## Page 53

53
induces identity maps on A and B and is also compatible with the natural sections
A →E and A →Eτ. Thus our two split extensions are equivalent.
□
Remark 7.38. Notice that the ideal B is essential in the split extension B ↣E ↠A
if and only if the associated ∗-homomorphism τ : A →M(B) satisfies τ −1(B) = 0,
that is, if the composition η := q ◦τ : A →Q(B) := M(B)/B is injective, where
q: M(B) ↠Q(B) denotes the quotient map.
Proposition 7.39. Let B and A be two Fell bundles over G. There is a natural
bijection between equivalence classes of split Fell-bundle extensions

Proof. First, assume that a split Fell-bundle extension is given. Then the contin-

multiplication in E defines a multiplier homomorphism A →M(B). Conversely, let
φ: A →M(B) be a multiplier homomorphism. Then we define
Eg := {(a, m) ∈Ag ⊕M(B)g : φg(a) −m ∈Bg}.
These subspaces are closed in norm and closed under the multiplication and involution
in A ⊕M(B). We give E the topology defined by the Banach space isomorphisms
Eg →Ag ⊕Bg, (a, b) 7→(a, φg(a) −b). Routine computations show that this makes
E := (Eg)g∈G a Fell bundle over G. The subspaces 0 ⊕Bg form a Fell ideal that
is naturally isomorphic to B, and the resulting quotient Fell bundle is isomorphic
to A by the projection (a, b) 7→a. The resulting Fell-bundle extension B ↣E ↠A
splits by the Fell-bundle homomorphism A →E, a 7→(a, φg(a)). Thus a multiplier
homomorphism determines a split Fell-bundle extension. The two constructions
above are easily seen to be inverse to each other up to a canonical isomorphism of
split Fell-bundle extensions.
□
Proposition 7.39 implies that a multiplier homomorphism also produces right

b1 · (b2 · a) = (b1 · b2) · a, a1 · (a2 · b) = (a1 · a2) · b, a1 · (b · a2) = (a1 · b) · a2,
b · (a1 · a2) = (b · a1) · a2, if a, aj ∈A, b, bj ∈B are composable. All this follows by
computing in the split extension E defined by the multiplier homomorphism. The
right multiplication maps Bg × Ah →Bgh may be defined directly by

Remark 7.46. The analogue of Proposition 7.45 fails for full section algebras. This
already happens for actions of groups on C∗-algebras. Let G be a nonamenable
locally compact group and let A = C(G+), the algebra of continuous functions
on the one-point compactification G+ = G ⊔{∞}, with the natural action of G
by translation on G and fixing ∞. Let I = C0(G) ◁A, which is a G-invariant
essential ideal. Then I ⋊max G = I ⋊r G, whereas A ⋊max G ̸= A ⋊r G because G is
nonamenable. If I ⋊max G were essential in A ⋊max G, the canonical map
A ⋊max G →M(I ⋊max G)
would be injective; but since I ⋊max G = I ⋊r G, this would force A⋊max G = A⋊r G,
a contradiction. Thus full crossed products do not preserve essential ideals. This
cannot get better for full section algebras of Fell bundles.
Let us now study when multiplier morphisms induce faithful ∗-homomorphisms
between Fell section C∗-algebras.
Definition 7.47. A multiplier homomorphism τ : A →M(B) is called faithful if
τg : Ag →M(B)g is isometric for all g ∈G.
Lemma 7.48. A multiplier homomorphism τ : A →M(B) is faithful if and only if
the ∗-homomorphisms τu(x) : Ax →M(Bx) are injective for all x ∈G0.
Proof. The isomorphism M(B)u(x) ∼= M(Bx) is observed in Remark 7.25. It is
trivial that τu(x) for x ∈G0 must be injective if τ is faithful. Conversely, assume
that τu(x) is injective for x ∈G0. Since τu(x) are ∗-homomorphisms, this makes
them isometric. Then the C∗-condition ∥a∗a∥= ∥a∥2 for a ∈M(B)g implies that τg
is isometric as well.
□

The result above does not extend to induced ∗-homomorphisms on full section
C∗-algebras; it already fails for ordinary morphisms A →B, even when A and B
are Fell bundles arising from group actions by automorphisms. The issue is that
a group G is amenable if and only if C∗(G) = C∗
r(G), while it is possible for a
non-amenable group to act amenably on a (say, unital) C∗-algebra A (see [1]). In
this case, the obvious homomorphism C →A, λ 7→λ · 1, which induces a morphism
of Fell bundles from the trivial action of G on C to the action of G on A. On the
level of reduced crossed products, the induced map C∗
r(G) →A ⋊r G is injective.
However, since G is not amenable, the canonical map C∗(G) →A ⋊G = A ⋊r G
fails to be injective. This shows that the full crossed product functor A 7→C∗(G, A)
does not, in general, preserve injectivity of morphisms of Fell bundles.
7.6. Approximate units. Approximate units for convolution ∗-algebras associated
to groupoids play a fundamental rôle in questions of nondegeneracy, representation
theory, and functoriality. We are going to carry the approximate units constructed
in Muhly–Renault–Williams [44] over to the general Fell bundle case. For saturated
and separable Fell bundles, the existence of such approximate units was established
by Muhly and Williams in [45, Proposition 5.1]. Extending this construction to
arbitrary Fell bundles is useful, for instance, to prove that multiplier morphisms are
already nondegenerate at the Cc-level or to apply the Cohen–Hewitt Factorisation
Theorem to representations of L1
I(A). The existence of a two-sided approximate unit
in Cc(G) is asserted in [44, Corollary 2.11], but only under the standing assumption
that the unit space G0 be paracompact. A closer inspection of the proof shows,
however, that this assumption is redundant:

8. Dual groupoid and quasi-orbit space
One important question about the section C∗-algebra C∗(G, A) for a Fell bundle A
over G concerns its ideal structure. In this section, we prove some basic results
about ideals in C∗(G, A), using the theory developed in [39]. The results here
are far from a complete answer. We define the dual groupoid of a Fell bundle
over G and the quasi-orbit space of this dual groupoid. We prove that there is a
continuous map from the primitive ideal space of C∗(G, A) to the quasi-orbit space
as in [39, Theorem 7.17]; the difference to the results in [39] is that we now allow
possibly nonsaturated Fell bundles over locally compact groupoids. Fell bundles
over locally compact groups are already considered in [39, Section 6.2]. There is
only one missing ingredient in the proof of [39, Theorem 7.17]. Namely, the proof of
[39, Proposition 7.16] uses Renault’s Disintegration Theorem. We explain how our
universal property may be used instead to complete this proof. Our discussion here
is not selfcontained. We only give basic definitions and explain how the information
that is missing in [39] follows from our universal property.
As before, let (G, α) be a locally compact groupoid with Haar system and let A
be a Fell bundle over G. Let A be the restriction of A to units, which is a field of
C∗-algebras over G0. Let A := C0(G0, A) be its C∗-algebra of C0-sections.
8.1. The dual groupoid and the quasi-orbit space. Let bA denote the set of
unitary equivalence classes of irreducible representations of A. We equip bA with
the usual topology, whose open subsets are those of the form bI for ideals I ◁A. We
are going to define a partial action of G on bA. This has a transformation groupoid,
which we call the dual groupoid of the Fell bundle. The quasi-orbit space of this
groupoid is called the quasi-orbit space of the Fell bundle.
For many purposes, we may replace bA by the primitive ideal space of A. This
gives the same quasi-orbit space. It makes a big difference, however, for the isotropy
of the dual groupoid. And the version with bA gives finer information about the ideal
structure of the crossed product (see [38,40]). This is why we work with bA here.

the Fell bundle A over G.
The following lemma relates the dual groupoid to invariant ideals and Fell ideals:
Lemma 8.1. An ideal I ◁A is invariant if and only if the corresponding open
subset bI of bA is invariant for the dual groupoid bA ⋊G.

representation of Is(g) ∩A∗

The map I 7→bI identifies the lattice of ideals I(A) with the lattice of open subsets
O( bA). By Lemma 8.1, this lattice isomorphism restricts to a bijection between the
lattices of A-invariant ideals in A and G-invariant, open subsets of bA. The latter is,
in turn, isomorphic to the lattice of open subsets of the quasi-orbit space bA/∼.
The quasi-orbit space does not change when we replace bA by Prim(A) everywhere
above. This is because the lattices of open subsets of bA and Prim(A) are both
isomorphic to the lattice of ideals in A.
8.2. Induction and restriction of ideals. Let A and B be C∗-algebras. We are
mainly interested in the case A = C0(G0, A) and B = C∗(G, A). It is instructive,
however, to work in greater generality for a while.
A homomorphism ι: A →M(B) allows to induce ideals from A to B and to
restrict ideals from B to A (see [39]). Namely, the induced ideal i(I) for an ideal
I ◁A is the closed linear span B · I · B of b1 · x · b2 for b1, b2 ∈B, x ∈I. And the
restricted ideal r(J) for an ideal J ◁B is the kernel of the composite homomorphism
A →M(B) →M(B/J). Equivalently, a ∈A belongs to r(J) if and only if a·B ⊆J,
if and only if B · a ⊆J. If ι(A) ⊆B, then r(J) = J ∩A. The two maps i and r
between the lattices of ideals in A and B form a Galois connection, that is, I ⊆r(J)
if and only if i(I) ⊆J (see [39, Lemma 2.8]). Many consequences of this property
are listed in [39, Proposition 2.9].
Let I(A) and I(B) denote the lattices of ideals in A and B, respectively. Let
IA(B) := {i(I) : I ∈I(A)} ⊆I(B),
IB(A) := {r(J) : J ∈I(B)} ⊆I(A)
be the sublattices of induced ideals in B and of restricted ideals in A, respectively.
Then i ◦r is a retraction from I(B) onto IA(B) and r ◦i is a retraction from I(A)
onto IB(A). And i and r restrict to lattice isomorphisms between IA(B) and IB(A)
that are inverse to each other. Our results about the ideal structure of B are really
results about these isomorphic lattices IA(B) ∼= IB(A). That is, we only describe
the induced ideals in B, not all ideals.
Proposition 8.2. Let (φ, U) be a representation of the Fell bundle A = (Ag)g∈G.
Then I = ker(φ) is an invariant ideal of A.

Theorem 8.4. Let A be a Fell bundle over a locally compact Hausdorff groupoid G
with Haar system α. Let A := C0(G0, A) and B := C∗(G, A). An ideal in A is
restricted from B if and only if it is invariant. An ideal in B is induced from A if
and only if it is of the form C∗(G, I) for a Fell ideal I in A. There is a continuous
map ϱ: bB →bA/∼so that the induced map on open subsets,
ϱ−1 : O( bA/∼) →O( bB) ∼= I(B),
is a bijection onto the subset IA(B) of induced ideals.
Proof. The machinery in [39] already shows most of this theorem. Only one key
result is missing, namely, that an ideal in A is restricted if and only if it is invariant.
As we will show below, Proposition 8.2 implies that restricted ideals are invariant.
And Proposition 7.2 implies as in [39] that invariant ideals are restricted.
First, we show that the ideal r(J) in A for an ideal J in B is invariant. We
interpret the quotient map π: B →B/J as a representation of B on the Hilbert
module B/J over B/J.
By the universal property of B, this is associated to
a representation (φ, U) of the Fell bundle A. The construction of the universal
representation in Section 7.1 shows that φ = π ◦ι for the canonical inclusion map
ι: A →M(B). So r(J) = ker φ. This ideal in A is invariant by Proposition 8.2.
Second, we show that any invariant ideal I is restricted from B. By Corollary 7.16,
I corresponds to a Fell ideal I in A. There is a quotient Fell bundle A/I. Its
restriction to units is A/I.
So the map A/I →M(C∗(G, A/I)) is faithful by
Proposition 7.2, applied to the Fell bundle A/I. Let J be the kernel of the canonical
quotient map C∗(G, A) →C∗(G, A/I). Then r(J) = I (compare the proof of
[39, Lemma 7.14]).
□
9. Representations on separable Hilbert spaces
Now we specialise to the case where D = C, so that F is a Hilbert space. As
before, let (G, α) be a locally compact groupoid with Haar system and let A be
a Fell bundle over G. Let A := A|G0 be the restriction of A to units, which is
a field of C∗-algebras over G0. We are going to define measurable Fell-bundle
representations on measurable fields of Hilbert spaces over G0 and turn these into
Fell-bundle representations on Hilbert spaces as in Definition 4.5. Under separability
assumptions, we are going to prove that, conversely, any Fell-bundle representation
comes from a measurable Fell-bundle representation in this way, which is unique
up to unitary equivalence almost everywhere. When combined with the universal
property of C∗(G, A), this gives Renault’s Disintegration Theorem.
The equivalence between Fell-bundle representations and measurable Fell-bundle
representations as defined below relies mainly on the well-known representation
theory of commutative C∗-algebras. In addition, we use ideas of Ramsay from [48,49]
to change the exceptional null sets that occur in the construction.
Let ν be a measure on G0 and let H = (Hx)x∈G0 be a ν-measurable field of
Hilbert spaces over G0 (we use the definition in [18, Definition 1 on p. 164], which
is repeated in [17, A69]). In particular, all Hilbert spaces Hx are assumed separable.
This will be a standard assumption throughout this section. We assume that Hx ̸= 0
for ν-almost all x ∈G0. If this fails, then we restrict ν to the set of points x ∈G0
with Hx ̸= 0. This leaves the Hilbert space L2(G0, H, ν) unchanged.
Definition 9.1. A measurable Fell-bundle pre-representation of A on H consists of
linear maps Sg : Ag →B(Hs(g), Hr(g)) for all g ∈G with the following properties:
(1) if (g, h) ∈G2, a1 ∈Ag, a2 ∈Ah, then Sg(a1)Sh(a2) = Sgh(a1 · a2);
(2) if g ∈G, a ∈Ag, then Sg(a)∗= Sg−1(a∗);
(3) if x ∈G0, the linear span of Su(x)(a)ξ for a ∈Ax, ξ ∈Hx is dense in Hx;

Conditions (1)–(3) in Definition 9.1 imply that Su(x) – which we also denote by
Sx – is a nondegenerate ∗-representation of Ax on Hx for all x ∈G0.
In order to define Fell-bundle representations, we need a condition on the mea-

integration map is the composite of the integration maps for ν and α. That is,

Definition 9.2. A measure µ on G is called symmetric if it is equivalent to inv∗µ,
its pushforward along the inversion map. A measure ν on G0 is called quasi-invariant
if ν ◦α is symmetric.
The definition of ˜α implies inv∗(ν ◦α) = ν ◦˜α. Thus ν is quasi-invariant if and
only if the measures ν ◦α and ν ◦˜α on G are equivalent.
The disintegration of representations of groupoids without Fell bundles involves
only quasi-invariant measures (see [51]). This remains true for saturated Fell bundles.
But it becomes wrong for Fell bundles that are not saturated:
Example 9.3. Let G be a locally compact Hausdorff étale groupoid and let A be an
upper semicontinuous field of C∗-algebras over G0. Since G is étale, there is an upper
semicontinuous field of Banach spaces A over G with u∗A = A and A|G\u(G0) = 0.
This becomes a Fell bundle over G with the product and involution from A. A
representation of this Fell bundle is the same as a representation of C0(G0, A). When
we disintegrate such a representation, then any measure ν on G0 may occur. It
need not be G-quasi-invariant because the Fell bundle A only sees G0 and not G.
This example suggests to consider the quasi-invariance of the measure ν ◦α on
an appropriate subset of G. The following lemma helps to make this precise:
