Remark 9.7. We are going to prove that a Fell-bundle representation on a separable
Hilbert space is equivalent to a measurable Fell-bundle representation with a measure
class that is quasi-invariant relative to this representation. Let us point out why
a rather obvious idea to find a counterexample to this does not work. As our
Fell bundle, we take the direct sum of a saturated Fell bundle, say, over a group,
and a Fell bundle that is living on units. Then a representation is just a pair of
representations of the two Fell bundles. The first representation gives a measure
class that is quasi-invariant in the usual sense and a field of Hilbert spaces for which
supp AA∗r∗H = G is possible, but the second representation may have an arbitrary
measure class. So why should the direct sum representation have a measure class
that is relatively quasi-invariant? What saves us is that the bundle of Hilbert spaces
and the measure classes for a direct sum of two Hilbert space representations are not
just the naive sums of the measurable representations and the measure classes. For
instance, if the two measure classes are orthogonal, that is, supported on disjoint
measurable subsets, then we should first restrict our fields of Hilbert spaces to these
disjoint subsets before adding them. This changes the supports of both fields of
Hilbert spaces defined above, and it turns out on inspection that the direct sum
representation does indeed come from a measurable Fell-bundle representation and
a measure class that is quasi-invariant relative to it.
Definition 9.8. A measurable Fell-bundle representation of A consists of a triple
(ν, H, S) where ν is a measure on the space G0 of units, H is a measurable field of
Hilbert spaces, S = (Sg)g∈G is a Fell-bundle pre-representation of A on H and the
measure ν is quasi-invariant relative to (Sur(g))g∈G.

Remark 9.16. For a saturated Fell bundle, the support supp AA∗r∗H is conull in G
and Σ is conull in G2. So the function ∆is almost everywhere defined and almost
everywhere multiplicative. This is what is called a weak homomorphism in [49]. One
of the main results in [49], Theorem 3.2, says that any such weak homomorphism is
almost everywhere equal to a strict homomorphism. Since ∆is only well defined up
to equality almost everywhere, we may assume that it is already chosen strict. This
gives a choice of ∆that is defined for all g ∈G and satisfies ∆(gh) = ∆(g)∆(h) for
all (g, h) ∈G2. If the bundle is not saturated, such an improvement of ∆seems

9.2. Formulation of the main result. Now we are going to turn a Fell-bundle
representation on a Hilbert space into a measurable Fell-bundle representation. This
corresponds to Renault’s Disintegration Theorem. We first formulate the theorem
that we are going to prove eventually:
Definition 9.18. Let Y be a locally compact space and ν a measure on Y . A
subset X ⊆Y is called ν-conull if it is ν-measurable and satisfies ν(Y \ X) = 0.
Our universal property in Theorem 4.6 together with the following theorem
generalises Renault’s Disintegration Theorem from [51] to nonsaturated separable
Fell bundles over second countable locally compact Hausdorff groupoids:
Theorem 9.19. Let G be a second countable, locally compact, Hausdorff groupoid.
Let α be a Haar system on G and let A be a Fell bundle over G such that Ax is
separable for all x ∈G0.
(1) Any Fell-bundle representation of (G, A, α) on a separable Hilbert space
may be obtained from a measurable Fell-bundle representation. That is,
it is the representation on L2(G0, H, ν) defined by a measure ν on G0, a
ν-measurable field of Hilbert spaces H with Hx ̸= 0 for ν-almost all x ∈G0,
and a measurable pre-representation S = (Sg)g∈G of A on H, such that ν is
quasi-invariant relative to S.
(2) Let νj, Hj, Sj for j = 1, 2 be as in (1). The corresponding Fell-bundle
representations on L2(G0, Hj, νj) are isomorphic if and only if ν1 and ν2
are equivalent and there are a [ν1]-conull subset X ⊆G0 and a measurable
family of unitaries Vx : H1,x
∼

Vr(g)S1,g(a)ξ for all g ∈G with s(g), r(g) ∈X, a ∈Ag, ξ ∈H1,s(g); here
[ν1] denotes the measure class of ν1.
Even more, any isomorphism of
Fell-bundle representations is of the form

We will prove the theorem by analysing a Fell-bundle representation (φ, U) of A
on a separable Hilbert space L. First, we discuss the separability assumptions.
Lemma 9.21. If Ax is separable for all x ∈G0, then Ag is separable for all g ∈G.
Proof. A Hilbert module E over a C∗-algebra B is countably generated if and only
if the C∗-algebra K(E) is σ-unital (see [41]). This implies that any Hilbert bimodule
between two separable C∗-algebras is countably generated as a right Hilbert module
and hence separable.
□
If G is second countable and Ag is separable for all g ∈G, then C0(G, A) is
separable. Then L2(G, A, s, ˜α) and L2(G, AA∗, r, α) are separable as well. Simi-
larly, C0(G2, d∗
jA) and C0(G2, v∗
j A) are separable, and so are the correspondences
involving G2. Summing up, all the Banach spaces that occur in the definition of a
Fell-bundle representation of A on L are separable.
9.3. Representations of fields of C∗-algebras. Next, we recall basic results
about representations of commutative C∗-algebras.
Theorem 9.22. Let X be a second countable, locally compact space and let L be a
separable Hilbert space. Let ϱ: C0(X) →B(L) be a nondegenerate representation.
Then there are a measure ν on X and a ν-measurable field of Hilbert spaces H =
(Hx)x∈X over X such that Hx ̸= 0 for ν-almost all x ∈X and ϱ is unitarily
equivalent to the representation of C0(X) on the Hilbert space L2(X, H, ν) of ν-
square-integrable sections of the field H by pointwise multiplication.
Proof. This follows from results in [19]. First, we may arrange that the represen-
tation ϱ is faithful: otherwise, its kernel is C0(U) for an open subset U ⊆X and
then we may replace X by the closed subspace X \ U, which corresponds to the
quotient C0(X)/C0(U). Thus ϱ identifies C0(X) with an Abelian C∗-subalgebra
of B(L). The nondegeneracy of ϱ says that idL is in the weak closure of ϱ(C0(X)).
Since L is separable, this representation has a “basic” measure by [19, Proposition 4
in Section I.7 on p. 116]. And then [19, Theorem 1 in Section II.6 on p. 208] implies
all the claims.
□
We also need information about the uniqueness of this disintegration:
Theorem 9.23. Let X be a second countable, locally compact space. For j = 1, 2,
let νj be measures on X and let Hj = (Hj,x)x∈X be νj-measurable fields of Hilbert
spaces over X such that Hj,x ̸= 0 νj-almost everywhere for j = 1, 2. There is a
unitary intertwiner V between the pointwise multiplication representations of C0(X)
on L2(X, Hj, νj) for j = 1, 2 if and only if [ν1] = [ν2] and the measurable fields of

Remark 9.25. The need to assume Hx ̸= 0 for ν-almost all x ∈X is overlooked in [9].
This assumption ensures uniqueness of the measure class of ν by Theorem 9.22. If ν
and H do not satisfy this, then we may replace ν by its restriction to the ν-measurable
subset {x ∈X : Hx ̸= 0} without changing the Hilbert space L2(X, H, ν). Then the
important claim [ν1] = [ν2] in Theorem 9.23 breaks down.

9.6. Third stage of the proof: invariant null sets in the saturated case.
So far, we have found a conull subset X0 ⊆G0 and changed the maps Sg on a null
set so that Sg is a measurable representation of G|X0 = {g ∈G : r(g), s(g) ∈X0}.
We have also disintegrated a unitary intertwiner and shown that the intertwining
condition holds for all g ∈G|X0. Of course, two unitary intertwiners are equal if
and only if their fibreweise operators are equal on a conull subset x ∈X0 ⊆G0.
What is still missing is to replace the exceptional set X0 by a G-invariant one.
In this subsection, we will achieve this under the assumption that the Fell bundle is
saturated. Having done that, we may redefine Hx = 0 outside X0 and so get rid
of the exceptional set for S altogether (but the exceptional sets do not go away
for intertwiners or their equality). The key idea in this section goes back to [49],
another article by Ramsay. Note that he calls G-invariant subsets “saturated”.
Since G is second countable, it is σ-compact. Then [49, Lemma 3.1] provides
a G-invariant ν-conull subset Z ⊆G0 and a measurable map η: Z →G with
s ◦η = idZ, r ◦η(Z) ⊆X0 ∩Z, and η(x) = u(x) for all x ∈X0 ∩Z. The maps
x 7→r ◦η(x)

for trivial reasons on G0 \ Z because Z is G-invariant. This proves all statements in
Theorem 9.19.(1) for saturated Fell bundles.

9.7. Fourth stage of the proof: removing the null set in general. From now
on, we again allow Fell bundles that are not saturated. We want to make the conull
set X0 ⊆G0 on which things work G-invariant in this case as well. The key idea in
order to generalise the argument in Section 9.6 is to use a sequence of local sections
of the range map that together cover a fibrewise dense subset of G. To build these
local sections, we first choose a countable basis (Wn)n∈N≥1 for the topology of G.
This is possible because G is assumed second countable.
Lemma 9.41 (compare [49, Lemma A]). There is a σ-compact ν-conull subset
contained in X.
Proof. Since G0 is second countable, G0 = S Kn with compact subsets Kn. Since
the measure ν is regular, for each n ∈N there is an increasing sequence of compact
subsets Ln,k ⊆Kn ∩X with ν(Kn ∩X \ Ln,k) →0. Then Z := S S
n,k Ln,k is
σ-compact, contained in X, and satisfies ν(X \ Z) = 0.
□
In the arguments above, we found that the statements we want hold on X or
on G|X for some ν-conull subset X ⊆G0. We replace X by a σ-compact ν-conull
subset as in Lemma 9.41 to arrange for X itself to be σ-compact. Let GX := {g ∈
G : s(g) ∈X}. This subset of G is σ-compact as well because G is countable at
infinity and X is σ-compact. For each n ∈N, let Rn := r(GX ∩Wn) ⊆G0; this is
σ-compact as well. The restriction of the range map to GX ∩Wn has a Borel section
ηn : Rn →GX ∩Wn
by the Federer–Morse Lemma [27, Theorem 5.1]. The subset Rn ∩X is σ-compact
as well, and we may assume without loss of generality that ηn(x) = u(x) for
all x ∈Rn ∩X, by changing ηn accordingly on Rn ∩X. It is convenient to let
η0(x) := u(x): W0 →G0 be the unit section X →GX.

This completes the construction of a measurable Fell-bundle representation
associated to a given Fell-bundle representation on a separable Hilbert space. This
finishes the proof of Theorem 9.19.(1). The proofs of Theorem 9.19.(2) and 9.19.(3)
were already finished in Section 9.5 because these statements do not assert that
we may make the conull subset in G0 G-invariant in general. The theorem only
asserts that this may be done in the saturated case, and this has also been shown
in Section 9.6. So we have finished the proof of Theorem 9.19. We do not know
whether the conull sets in G0 in Theorem 9.19.(2) and 9.19.(3) can always be chosen
G-invariant, also for nonsaturated Fell bundles.

10. Twisted partial actions
In this section, we consider a Fell bundle A that is associated to a twisted partial
action. The latter consists of a continuous family of ideals Dg ⊆Ar(g), a continuous
family of ∗-isomorphisms ag : Dg−1
∼
−→Dg for g ∈G, and a continuous family of
unitary multipliers w(g, h) of ah(Dg ∩Dh−1) for (g, h) ∈G2, which satisfy the
conditions in Definition 3.22. The resulting Fell bundle is built in Definition 3.25.
We first identify measurable Hilbert space representations of A with measurable
covariant representations. Second, we prove an analogous result for representations
on Hilbert modules, which does not need separability. This only works, however, if
the action is global, not partial, and the twist is scalar-valued.
Definition 10.1. A measurable covariant representation of a twisted partial action
has the data in (1)–(4) subject to the conditions (5)–(8):
(1) ν is a measure class on G0;
(2) H = (Hx)x∈G0 is a measurable field of Hilbert spaces with Hx ̸= 0 for
ν-almost all x ∈G0;
(3) φx : Ax →B(Hx) is a measurable family of nondegenerate representations
for all x ∈G0;
(4) Ug : φs(g)(Dg−1)Hs(g)

(5) ν is quasi-invariant relative to the family of representations (φx)x∈G0;
(6) Ugφs(g)(a)U ∗
g = φr(g)(ag(a)) for all g ∈G and a ∈Dg−1;
(7) if (g, h) ∈G2, then UgUh is a restriction of φr(g)(w(g, h))Ugh;
(8) (φx)x∈G0 and (Ug)g∈G are measurable in the sense that pointwise application
of φx(ax) for a ∈A and of Ug map measurable sections to measurable
sections.

Proposition 10.8. Assume that our Fell bundle comes from a global twisted action.
Then a measurable covariant representation has the following data:
(1) a measure class ν on G0;
(2) a measurable field of Hilbert spaces H = (Hx)x∈G0 with Hx ̸= 0 for ν-almost
all x ∈G0;
(3) a measurable family of nondegenerate representations φx : Ax →B(Hx) for
all x ∈G0;
(4) unitary operators Ug : Hs(g)
∼

this is subject to the following conditions:
(5) ν is quasi-invariant;
(6) Ugφs(g)(a)U ∗
g = φr(g)(ag(a)) for all g ∈G;
(7) if (g, h) ∈G2, then UgUh = φr(g)(w(g, h))Ugh;
(8) (φx)x∈G0 and (Ug)g∈G are measurable in the sense that pointwise application
of φx(ax) for a ∈A and of Ug map measurable sections to measurable
sections.
Proof. The assumption that the twisted action is global means that Dg = Ar(g)
for all g ∈G and therefore the corresponding Fell bundle is saturated. Therefore,
the measure ν is quasi-invariant in the usual, absolute sense. In addition, the
unitaries Ug in a covariant representation are unitaries Hs(g)
∼
−→Hr(g) because
φr(x)(Dg)Hr(g) = Hr(g) for all g ∈G. This simplifies the data in Definition 10.1 to
the data listed in this proposition.
□
If the twist is trivial, then (7) in Proposition 10.8 simplifies to UgUh = Ugh, so
that g 7→Ug becomes a groupoid representation.
In general, we do not know how to define “covariant representations” of a twisted
partial action on a Hilbert module because we cannot disintegrate U to a family of
fibrewise unitaries Ug. We can only define covariant representations if the action
is global, that is, Dg = Ar(g) for all g ∈G, and the twist is scalar-valued, that
is, w(g, h) ∈U(C) ⊆U(Ar(g)) for all (g, h) ∈G2. In this case, the action satisfies
agah = agh for all (g, h) ∈G2, and the twist together with the trivial action defines
a twisted action on C0(G0). We turn this into a Fell line bundle L over G.
Definition 10.9. Let A be an upper semicontinuous field of C∗-algebras over G0,

representation of L and

11. Fell line bundles as twisted groupoids
Next, we consider representations of a twisted groupoid C∗-algebra. We describe
a twist of a groupoid G through a Fell line bundle L = (Lg)g∈G over G. This is
equivalent to another description through a circle extension of G. Namely, let Σ ⊆L
be the subset of unitaries in the Fell line bundle. If y ∈Lg for some g ∈G, then the
conditions y∗y = 1 in Ls(g) ∼= C and yy∗= 1 in Lr(g) ∼= C are equivalent. Therefore,
Σ =

g∈G
Lg : y∗y = 1 ∈Ls(g) ∼= C

.
The subset Σ with the multiplication and the topology of the Fell bundle and the
range and source maps s(y) = s(g) and r(y) = r(g) for y ∈Lg becomes a topological
groupoid. The circle group T acts freely on Σ through the scalar multiplication in
the Fell bundle, such that Σ/T ∼= G.
Theorem 11.1. Let L be a Fell line bundle over G. There is a bijection between
measurable representations of L and T-equivariant measurable representations of Σ.
Proof. Consider a measurable representation (ν, (Hx)x∈G0, (Sg)g∈G) of the Fell line
bundle L. By definition, each Sg is a bounded linear map Lg →B(Hs(g), Hr(g)).
Since Lg ∼= C, such a map is determined by its restriction to the unitary elements
in Lg. Define Uy := Sg(y) for y ∈Σ ∩Lg, g ∈G. Since y is unitary, Sg(y)Sg(y)∗=
Sg(y)Sg−1(y∗) = Sr(g)(yy∗) = idHr(g) and, similarly, Sg(y)∗Sg(y) = idHs(g). As a
result, we get unitaries Uy : Hs(y) →Hr(y) for all y ∈Σ. These unitaries, together
with the same ν and H, form a measurable representation of the groupoid Σ; it is
easy to see that ν is G-quasi-invariant as a measure class on G0 if and only if it is
Σ-quasi-invariant. In addition, the unitaries Uy satisfy the T-equivariance condition
Uz·y = z · Uy for all z ∈T, y ∈Σ. Conversely, a measurable representation of Σ that

is T-equivariant in this sense must come from a measurable representation of the

We are going to improve this statement to an isomorphism of C∗-algebras. This
uses a circle action on C∗(Σ). For z ∈T, define a linear map on the ∗-algebra Cc(Σ)
by (µzf)(x) := f(z−1x).
Lemma 11.2. Each µz is a central unitary multiplier of Cc(Σ) and extends to a
central unitary multiplier of C∗(Σ). The map z 7→µz is strictly continuous.
Proof. We compute
µz(f1 ∗f2) = µz(f1) ∗f2 = f1 ∗µz(f2)
because z−1 · (x · y) = (z−1 · x) · y = x · (z−1 · y) for all z ∈T, x, y ∈Σ with
s(x) = r(y). This says that µz is a central unitary multiplier of Cc(Σ). The map
µz : Cc(Σ) →Cc(Σ) is bounded in the I-norm and continuous in the inductive
limit topology. If ∥⌞⌟∥: Cc(Σ) →[0, ∞) is any C∗-norm that is bounded in the
I-norm, then ∥µz(f)∥= ∥µz(f)∗µz(f)∥1/2 = ∥f ∗f∥1/2 = ∥f∥for all f ∈Cc(Σ).
This implies that µz extends to the groupoid C∗-algebra, and the extension remains
a central unitary multiplier. If f ∈Cc(Σ), then the map T →Cc(Σ), z 7→µz(f),
is continuous even in the inductive limit topology on Cc(Σ). The same is true for
µ∗
z(f) = µz−1(f). This implies that the map z 7→µz is strictly continuous.
□
The group homomorphism µ from T to the group of central unitary multipliers
of C∗(Σ) integrates to a ∗-homomorphism from C0(Z) ∼= C∗(T) to the centre of the
multiplier algebra of C∗(Σ). As a result, C∗(Σ) decomposes as a C0-direct sum of
the C∗-subalgebras
C∗(Σ)n := {f ∈C∗(Σ) : µz(f) = znf for all z ∈T}
for n ∈Z. Define Cc(Σ)n for n ∈Z similarly.

maximal C∗-seminorm that is bounded in the restriction of the I-norm.
Proof. The canonical projection En : C∗(Σ) →C∗(Σ)n, f 7→

C∗(Σ)n is the completion of Cc(Σ)n in the restriction of the norm of C∗(Σ). This
restriction is a C∗-seminorm that is bounded in the restricted I-norm. It remains to
show that it is the largest such seminorm. Since the map En is a ∗-homomorphism
and bounded for the I-norm, any C∗-seminorm on Cc(Σ)n that is bounded in the
restricted I-norm extends to a C∗-seminorm on Cc(Σ) that is still bounded in the
I-norm, and hence it is bounded by the norm on C∗(Σ).

A section f ∈Cc(G, L) defines a scalar-valued map ˜f : Σ →C by ˜f(x) = x∗· f(g)
for all g ∈G, x ∈Lg ∩Σ. The map ˜f is continuous and has compact support, and
it belongs to C∗(Σ)1 because
(µz ˜f)(x) = ˜f(z−1 · x) = (z−1 · x)∗· f(g) = z · x∗· f(g) = z · ˜f(x).
Thus we get a canonical map Cc(G, L) →Cc(Σ)1.
Theorem 11.4. The map Cc(G, L) →Cc(Σ)1 is a ∗-isomorphism. It extends to a
∗-isomorphism C∗(G, L) ∼= C∗(Σ)1.
Proof. It is easy to check that the map Cc(G, L) →Cc(Σ)1 is a ∗-homomorphism.
To see that it is bijective, take a function ˜f ∈Cc(Σ)1. Let g ∈G. Then z·x· ˜f(z·x) =
x· ˜f(x) for all x ∈Lg∩Σ, that is, f(g) := x· ˜f(x) ∈Lg is well defined. This defines an
element f ∈Cc(G, L) that maps to ˜f under the map above. The two constructions

easy to check for measurable Fell-bundle representations on Hilbert spaces.
12. Fell bundles over groups
This section studies Fell bundles and their section C∗-algebras over groups. Let G
be a locally compact group and let A be a Fell bundle over G. The Haar measure
on G is unique up to a scalar factor. So the section C∗-algebra of A does not
depend on it, and we denote it by C∗(G, A). The usual definition of C∗(G, A) for a
group G in [21] differs in two ways from ours. First, it uses the modular function
∆: G →(0, ∞) to define the adjoint. We define ∆so that d(g−1) = ∆(g−1) dg. The
usual formula for the involution on Cc(G, A) is f ×(g) := f(g−1)∗∆(g)−1 instead of
our formula f ∗(g) := f(g−1)∗. The map

(µf)∗= µ(f ×)
for all f1, f2, f ∈Cc(G, A). The second difference regards the L1-norm that we use
to bound representations on group C∗-algebras, or more generally group crossed
products or Fell bundles. The usual L1-norm and the I-norm are only related
through an inequality ∥f∥1 ≤∥µ(f)∥I, which follows from the Cauchy–Schwarz
inequality:

We also learn from the previous result that any representation of (Cc(G, A), ∗,× )
that is continuous in the inductive limit topology is already bounded for the usual
L1-norm. This is an analogue of Corollary 6.14 using the L1-norm instead of the
I-norm. For ordinary crossed products by locally compact group actions, this was
proved by Green in [32, Corollary to Proposition 3 on p. 203]. This is extended to
Fell bundles over locally compact groups in [21, Theorem VIII.13.8].
In the case of groups, there is an alternative definition of representations of
Fell bundles using maps A →B(F) that respect product and involution and are
continuous for the strict topology. More precisely, this is defined as follows:
Definition 12.2 (Fell). A continuous representation of A on a Hilbert module F
is a map π: A →B(F) which is linear on the fibres of A and satisfies
(1) π(a · b) = π(a)π(b) and π(a∗) = π(a)∗for all a, b ∈A;
(2) the representation π|A1 of A1 is nondegenerate;
(3) the map a 7→π(a) is continuous from A to B(F) = M(K(F)) with respect
to the strict topology.
Recall that the strict topology on B(F) is equivalent to the ∗-strong topology on
bounded sets. Using the compatibility with the involution in (1) it follows that the
continuity of π in (3) is equivalent to norm continuity of the map a 7→π(a)ξ for all
ξ ∈F, that is, strong continuity of π.
Continuous representations of Fell bundles were extensively studied by Fell, see
for instance [21] and references there in. Usually, only Hilbert space representations
are considered in the literature, but similar techniques apply to Hilbert module
representations as above. One of the main results in this direction states that
continuous representations correspond bijectively to representations of C∗(G, A).
Combined with our results, it follows that the continuous representations in the
sense of Fell must be equivalent to our representations. We are going to prove this
directly and deduce an automatic continuity result for measurable representations
on Hilbert spaces.
The key idea is that there is a universal continuous representation
m: A →M(C∗(G, A))
of the Fell bundle by multipliers of C∗(G, A). For g ∈G and a ∈Ag, we define
ma : Cc(G, A) →Cc(G, A) by

Theorem 12.5. Let G be a locally compact group and let A be a Fell bundle
over G. Let D be a C∗-algebra and let F be a Hilbert D-module. Then there is
a natural bijection between Fell-bundle representations of A on F and continuous
representations of A on F.
Proof. Theorem 4.6 gives a natural bijection between Fell-bundle representations
of A on F and nondegenerate representations of C∗(G, A). Any nondegenerate
representations of C∗(G, A) extends to multipliers, and composing with the universal
continuous representation m: A →M(C∗(G, A)) of A, it induces a continuous rep-
resentation of A on F. Conversely, let π: A →B(F) be a continuous representation.
Then it integrates to a representation of the L1-cross-section ∗-algebra and hence
also to its enveloping C∗-algebra C∗(G, A) (see [21]). The latter corresponds to a
Fell-bundle representation of A. In fact, we may directly describe the latter using π.
Let φ := π|A1 and define U : Cc(G, A) ⊙C(G, F) →Cc(G, F) by
(U(a ⊗f))(g) := π(a(g))f(g)
for a ∈Cc(G, A), f ∈Cc(G, F), g ∈G. We compute that U is isometric and thus
extends to an isometry U : L2(G, A) ⊗A1 F →F. This operator intertwines the
canonical actions of A1. Therefore, its image is contained in L2(G, AA∗) ⊗A1 F.
The multiplicativity of U implies by an easy computation that U is a Fell-bundle
representation.
□
Now we turn to measurable Fell-bundle representations on a Hilbert space. Here
the theory in Section 9 simplifies somewhat. The space G0 has just one point and
so disintegrations over G0 become empty. In particular, the measure class ν lives on
a one-point space, so that it becomes irrelevant, and the measurable field of Hilbert
spaces H becomes just the underlying Hilbert space of our representation. So a
measurable representation on H becomes just a map S : A →B(H) that preserves
the multiplication and the involution and is measurable in a suitable sense.
Theorem 12.6. Let G be a second countable, locally compact group and let A be a
separable Fell bundle over G. Let H be a separable Hilbert space. Any measurable
Fell-bundle representation of A on H is continuous.
Proof. By Theorem 9.19, a measurable Fell-bundle representation S = (Sg)g∈G of A
on H is equivalent to a Fell-bundle representation as in Definition 4.5. And this is
equivalent to a representation σ: C∗(G, A) →B(H). Let ¯σ: M(C∗(G, A)) →B(H)
be the unique extension of σ to multipliers. Composing this with the universal
continuous representation m: A →M(C∗(G, A)) defined in (12.3) gives a continuous

13. Transformation groupoids
Let a group or groupoid G act (partially) on a locally compact space X. Let
G ⋉X be the resulting transformation groupoid. As we shall see, a Fell bundle
over G ⋉X yields a Fell bundle over G, and these two Fell bundles have naturally
isomorphic representations and hence isomorphic section C∗-algebras. In particular,
this reproves that the groupoid C∗-algebra C∗(G ⋉X) is isomorphic to the crossed
product G ⋉C0(X).
The transformation groupoid G ⋉X has object space X, and its arrow space is
the space of triples (x, g, y) with x, y ∈X, g ∈G such that s(g) = r(y) and x = g ·y;
in particular, y is in the domain of the action of g. This is an extra condition in
the case of a partial action, which is allowed in this section. We do not give names
to the domains, however, and write “g · x is defined” instead. The multiplication
is defined by (x, g, y) · (y, h, z) = (x, g · h, z). Let α be a Haar system on G. This
induces a Haar system β on G ⋉X by
Z
(G⋉X)x f(x, g, g−1 · x) dβx(x, g, g−1 · x) :=
Z
f(x, g, g−1 · x) dαr(x)(g),
where the second integral is over the (open) set {g ∈Gr(x) : g−1 · x is defined}.
Let B be a Fell bundle over G ⋉X. It induces a Fell bundle A over G as follows.
Let
Xg := {(x, g, g−1x) ∈G ⋉X : x ∈X, r(x) = r(g), g−1x is defined}.
The fibre of A at g ∈G is Ag := C0(Xg, B|Xg), and the multiplication and involution
on A are defined by applying the multiplication and involution on B pointwise. The
topology is defined so that the C0-sections of A are equivalent to C0-sections of B.
More precisely, if f is a C0-section of B, then the corresponding continuous section
of A maps g ∈G to the restriction of f to Xg. This defines a Fell bundle over G.
Proposition 13.1. Let G be a locally compact groupoid with a Haar system α.
Let X be a locally compact space with a (partial) action of G. Let B be a Fell bundle
over G ⋉X. Define a Haar measure β on G ⋉X and a Fell bundle A over G as
above. Then
C∗(G, A) ∼= C∗(G ⋉X, B, β).
Proof. By definition, there is a canonical isomorphism C0(G, A) ∼= C0(G ⋉X, B).
It restricts to a canonical isomorphism between A := C0(G0, A) and B := C0(X, B).
It also follows that C0(G, AA∗) ∼= C0(G ⋉X, BB∗), and similarly for the spaces
of C0-sections on G2 and (G ⋉X)2 that we need. The Haar measures are related
in such a way that L2(G, A, s, ˜α) ∼= L2(G ⋉X, B, s, ˜β) and L2(G, AA∗, r, α) ∼=
L2(G⋉X, BB∗, r, β) as correspondences. And the correspondences L2(G2, Ek, vk, µk)
for k = 0, 1, 2 that occur in the representation conditions over G and G ⋉X are
canonically isomorphic as well. Using these isomorphisms, we identify the data and


## Page 93


A UNIVERSAL PROPERTY FOR GROUPOID C*-ALGEBRAS. II. FELL BUNDLES
93
the conditions for Fell-bundle representations of A and B on any Hilbert module F.
This induces a natural isomorphism between C∗(G, A) and C∗(G ⋉X, B, β) because
of their universal properties.
□
Example 13.2. When B is the trivial Fell bundle, then C∗(G ⋉X, B, β) = C∗(G ⋉X)
is the groupoid C∗-algebra of G ⋉X for our choice of Haar system. Proposition 13.1
identifies this with the section C∗-algebra of a Fell bundle over G. In fact, this
Fell bundle comes from the (partial) action of G on C0(X) induced by its (partial)
action on X as explained after Definition 3.22. So it is the (partial) crossed product
C0(X) ⋊G.
Remark 13.3. Without our universal property, Proposition 13.1 is not completely
trivial. While the C0-sections of the two bundles are canonically isomorphic, the
Cc-sections of the Fell bundles A and B are different unless the map X →G0 is
proper. This is visible already for the trivial Fell bundle and when G is a group acting
on a noncompact space X. The C∗-algebra C∗(G⋉X, B, β) is defined as a completion
of Cc(G ⋉X), whereas C∗(G, A) is defined as a completion of Cc(G, C0(X)). So
we partially completed the function space in the direction of X. Proposition 13.1
implies that any representation of the ∗-algebra Cc(G ⋉X) that is continuous in
the inductive limit topology over compact subsets of G ⋉X extends uniquely to a
representation of Cc(G, C0(X)) that is continuous in the inductive limit topology
over compact subsets of G. If G = {1}, we have to extend representations of Cc(X)
to C0(X). This is done in Lemma 6.2.
Remark 13.4. Results closely related to Proposition 13.1 have appeared earlier
in several special cases. In particular, decomposition results for crossed products
by transformation groupoids are discussed in detail, together with many further
examples and references, in [12].
The general framework developed there is that of groupoid fibrations and actions of
groupoids on groupoids by equivalences, which in particular covers global actions and
leads to saturated Fell bundles. From this point of view, transformation groupoids
arising from global actions fit naturally into this framework.
In the present section, however, we also allow partial actions, and the associated
transformation groupoids do not, in general, define groupoid fibrations as in [12].
Proposition 13.1 may therefore be viewed as an analogue of the decomposition
results for groupoid fibrations, extended to the setting of (possibly) partial actions,
even though no general notion of a “partial groupoid fibration” is developed here.
